A REAL, REPRODUCIBLE ANIMATION BUG, FOUND FROM A SCREENSHOT. `filter: blur(3px)` transitioning on a live text glyph (`.lp-kinetic-word`, added at v3.204.0) is a known-risky pattern — blur-on-text compositing is inconsistent across browsers/GPUs and can visibly distort a letterform mid-transition, which is exactly what the screenshot caught: the "S" in "Search" rendering as a smeared dash-and-swoosh instead of a letter. Confirmed by reloading repeatedly and catching mid-animation frames before touching anything — the artifact was real and reproducible, not a one-off. Fixed by removing the blur entirely; opacity plus a small vertical shift alone still reads as "reveals in" with zero risk of a letterform ever rendering as anything but a letter. Re-verified across several fresh reloads afterward, clean every time.

ASKED DIRECTLY WHAT WAS ACTUALLY MISSING RATHER THAN GUESSING AGAIN. Three passes in a row had all been read as still missing the point, so before making a fourth guess: asked which of persuasive writing, real content marketing, or a real visual redesign was the actual gap. Answered: all three, for both audiences, "a huge change that fits our strategy."

REAL, NEW MARKETING CONTENT: A DIRECT COMPARISON, NOT ANOTHER FEATURE LIST. New `HeadToHead.tsx` — a real head-to-head comparison block, deliberately a different visual shape from the `.lp-split` two-column pattern every other section on this page already uses, so it doesn't read as the same section repeated. Every line in it restates a claim this file's own copy already makes elsewhere (the PAIN section, `AI_CONTRAST`, `EASY_HIRING_CHIPS`) — nothing new is asserted, it's the same honest, already-defensible facts in the one format that actually persuades: named side by side, not described in the abstract. Seeker version: "Other job boards" vs AYN, five real points (ghost jobs, one resume for everything, no visibility into gaps, keyword-skimmed resumes, sponsored-listing pressure). Employer version: "A recruiter or staffing agency" vs AYN, four real points (placement-fee percentage, weeks of back and forth, an unsorted resume pile, unverified claims). Placed right after the PAIN section for both audiences — the highest-visibility position for a comparison to actually change a visitor's mind.

ONE MORE INVENTED PERSON REMOVED. `CandidateCardMockup` (used in the employer hero and the seeker "Get discovered" section) still showed a fabricated candidate named "Maya" with an invented 91 score and invented accomplishment sentences — the same class of problem `BrowseJobsMockup` was fixed for at v3.206.0, just not yet reached. Candidate data is genuinely private, unlike a job posting, so there's no live, anon-safe source to feed this the way `LiveJobsPreview` works for jobs. Redrawn as a labeled diagram instead: the same real structure (a score ring, why-they-matched, matched skills, stated gaps) stays, because that shape is true of every real card, but every specific fabricated value underneath it — the name, the score, the sentences — is now a plain, honest label describing what belongs there ("A candidate, anonymized," "A named, concrete accomplishment from their real history") instead of a specific invented instance of one.

Verified live for both audiences, not assumed from the code: confirmed the comparison renders correctly with the right label and all five/four real rows for job_seeker and employer respectively, and confirmed the employer hero's candidate card diagram no longer contains "Maya" or "91" anywhere. `npx tsc --noEmit`, eslint, and `npm run build` all clean.

NOT YET DONE, FLAGGED HONESTLY: four more mockups (`TailoredDocsMockup`, `ShortlistMockup`, `AssessmentMockup`, and `InboxMockup`, the last judged lower-priority since its example messages were already generic rather than tied to an invented name) still carry some fabricated content; a genuine visual-redesign pass (composition, not just component-by-component fixes) and real off-site content marketing assets (a salary/hiring-trend report, SEO guide content) are both still open from the same direction-setting answer.

Preceded by v3.207.0, the hero stopped showing a picture of search and became one. Reported directly, one message after v3.206.0 shipped: replacing a fake mockup with a real-data-fed one still wasn't the point — the landing page should work like a better version of Indeed, a real search a visitor can use immediately, not marketing copy next to a preview of the product.

THE HERO'S PRIMARY ACTION IS NOW A REAL SEARCH, NOT A BUTTON NEXT TO A CARD. New `HeroJobSearch.tsx`: two real fields, Indeed's own two-box pattern (job title/keyword, city or remote), submitting straight into the already-built, already-working `/jobs` public search — no new search logic, this just gave that existing page a real front door instead of a decorative preview standing in front of it. `PublicJobs.tsx` now reads `?q=`/`?where=` off the URL on mount and applies both (`where` as a proper `.ilike('location', ...)` AND-filter, kept separate from the free-text `q` match against title/company/location), with a second, visible "City or remote" input added next to the existing search box so the filter a visitor arrived with is transparent and editable, not a hidden query-string effect. `LiveJobsPreview.tsx` (last version's real-data fix) was rewritten again in the same pass: no more fake browser-chrome frame (the dots, the "Browse jobs" title bar) — reported directly that even a real-data-fed screenshot still reads as "a picture of the product," so the chrome is gone and the real postings just sit on the page as real content, each one a real link into `/jobs/:id`.

THE HEADLINE HAD TO STOP PROMISING SOMETHING A SEARCH ALONE DOESN'T GIVE BACK. "Every real job, scored against you" put a signed-in feature (a match score needs a resume on file) in front of the very first thing an anonymous visitor's search actually returns. Rewritten to "Search real jobs. Never a ghost listing." — accurate to what happens the instant someone searches, keeps the ghost-jobs hook from v3.204.0 front and center, and reads closer to Indeed's own plain, keyword-forward headline pattern than the previous, more clever one did. "Start free" (the account-creation CTA) moved from the hero's primary button to a secondary line under the search bar ("Want AYN to score every job against your resume automatically? Start free.") — search itself needs no account, matching the same free-first positioning `/jobs` and `/check-resume` already established; account creation is the upsell now, not the gate.

Also checked directly, per the same request: a full sweep for literal em/en dash characters across the whole site's own source, confirmed zero in the prior pass, re-confirmed clean after all of this pass's new copy too.

Verified live end to end against the real dev server: typed a real keyword and a real city into the two hero fields, submitted, landed on `/jobs?q=engineer&where=Toronto` with both fields correctly pre-filled from the URL, and confirmed the first five real results were all genuinely both -- every title containing "Engineer," every location containing "Toronto" ("Toronto," "Toronto, Ontario, Canada," "Toronto, ON") -- proving the two filters combine correctly, not just individually. `npx tsc --noEmit`, eslint, and `npm run build` all clean.

Preceded by v3.206.0, the hero's own product visual stopped inventing things. Asked directly, framed as a senior design pass: fix the landing page for real, and make sure the site never reads as AI-generated.

THE HERO WAS FABRICATING THE ONE THING THE PRODUCT PROMISES NEVER TO. Flagged in the design-audit research the round before, then acted on directly: the seeker hero's product visual (`BrowseJobsMockup`, `AppMockups.tsx`) was a hand-authored SVG showing invented companies ("Acme," "Halcyon," "Northline," "Cobalt") and invented match percentages, in Inter — not Figtree, the site's own real body font, and the exact face 2026 design research already flagged as the "this looks AI-generated" tell. AYN's entire pitch is "nothing invented." The one visual a first-time visitor sees before reading a word of copy was, until now, the one place on the whole site that broke that promise twice over: fake data, in the wrong typeface.

REPLACED WITH THE REAL THING, NOT A BETTER FAKE ONE. New `LiveJobsPreview.tsx`, fed by the exact same anon-readable, scam-excluded `job_postings` query `TrustBento.tsx` already proved out live: real company names, real titles, a real `NEW` badge computed the same way Browse Jobs' own `HOT_WINDOW_MS` already does, correctly in Figtree. Deliberately does NOT show a match score — there is no resume to score against for an anonymous visitor, and inventing a percentage here would have swapped one fabrication for another; freshness is a real, honest signal instead, true or false from `posted_at` alone. Used in both places the old mockup appeared: the hero itself, and the "Where jobs come from" section further down the same page, so the two don't disagree with each other the way a live one and a fake one would have. `BrowseJobsMockup` deleted outright once both call sites moved off it, confirmed via grep to have no other consumers.

NOT YET DONE, FLAGGED HONESTLY: the same invented-name pattern is still live in five more mockups in the same file (`TailoredDocsMockup` alone still shows "Acme, Senior Frontend Engineer" further down the page) — this pass fixed the single most visible, most-repeated one (the hero, shown twice), not all six. A real follow-up, not attempted here since each of the remaining five needs its own judgment call about what real or honestly-neutral content replaces the fabricated names, not a mechanical swap.

Also checked directly, per the same request: a full sweep for literal em dash and en dash characters across every `.tsx`/`.ts`/`.md` file and `index.html` found zero — the standing "no em dashes, no en dashes" house rule this app has enforced in every AI system prompt since early in its history already holds for the site's own authored copy. Nothing to fix there; confirmed rather than assumed.

Verified live against the real dev server, not assumed from the code: DOM-level inspection (this session's screenshot tool stayed unreliable on some tabs, confirmed unrelated to the change by cross-checking against a fresh tab and against `innerText` directly) confirmed both mockup instances rendering real, current company names and titles pulled live from the database (`black-mcdonald-limited`, `skyspecs`, `Ledgebrook`, none of them invented), correct `NEW` badges, and the two instances agreeing with each other. `npx tsc --noEmit`, eslint, and `npm run build` all clean.

Preceded by v3.205.0, category and location job pages (Tier 2 of the positioning brief), plus a real, sitewide SEO bug found and fixed while verifying them: every page on the site was carrying a duplicate or actively wrong canonical tag.

CATEGORY AND LOCATION PAGES, THE PROGRAMMATIC-SEO PLAYBOOK'S OWN RECIPE, BUILT ON DATA ALREADY SITTING THERE UNUSED. `job_postings.category`/`.city` have been captured since v3.166.0 and never had a page of their own. New routes `/jobs/category/:category` and `/jobs/location/:location`, both served by the same `PublicJobs.tsx` component `/jobs`/`/jobs/:id` already use (a filter added to `buildQuery`, not a new page), each with a real, unique `<h1>`, lead paragraph, and `<SEO>` title/description naming the actual category or city, not a generic template. `category` is used as-is (already a slug, freehire's own enrichment field / `ats-direct-sync`'s `toSlug()`); a city gets a plain slugify/deslugify pair since it isn't already one, safe here because the real values are clean plain names ("Austin," "New York City") with no punctuation to lose in the round trip. A "Browse:" strip of 10 real categories and 8 real cities on the plain `/jobs` hub — every one confirmed live against the actual table before being hardcoded, the smallest still carrying 150+ real rows — is the hub-and-spoke internal linking the research names as necessary, not just a URL that resolves if you already know it; each hub page links back via an "All jobs" breadcrumb. New `MIN_INDEXABLE_LISTINGS = 5`, the exact number the programmatic-SEO research names: a category/location page with fewer real listings than that renders normally for a human but gets `noIndex` on its `<SEO>` call, so a thin page never drags down the site's crawl-quality signal. 18 of these (the same curated set the browse strip links to) added to `public/sitemap.xml` directly — a fixed, stable, hand-verified list, not worth a second dynamic sitemap route the way the full per-job catalog already got one at v3.202.0.

A REAL BUG FOUND BUILDING THIS, FIXED BEFORE IT SHIPPED: JOBPOSTING SCHEMA WAS ABOUT TO LAND ON A LISTING PAGE. The existing `jsonLd` on `/jobs`/`/jobs/:id` keyed off `selected` — which auto-populates to the list's own first row even with no specific job in the URL, meaning the base `/jobs` hub (and every new category/location hub) was one line away from injecting real `JobPosting` structured data onto a page that isn't actually about one job. Google's own docs, already read once for this exact feature at v3.203.0, are explicit: "Google requires JobPosting schema on individual job pages only. Never on search results, category, or list pages." Fixed by gating `jsonLd` (and the SEO title/description/canonical) on `routeId` instead — true only when the URL itself is a real `/jobs/:id` — closing a gap that would have hit the existing pages too, not just the new ones.

THE REAL FIND OF THIS PASS: EVERY PAGE ON THE SITE HAD A DUPLICATE, AND SOMETIMES ACTIVELY WRONG, CANONICAL TAG. Verifying the new pages live (`document.querySelectorAll`, not a screenshot) turned up two static tags in `index.html` — `<link rel="canonical" href="https://ayn.careers/">` and a matching `<meta name="description">` — sitting in the served HTML on every route, unconditionally. `react-helmet-async` only ever manages tags it renders itself; it has no way to know a raw static tag baked into the HTML even exists, so it was never being replaced, only added alongside. Two real consequences: every page carried two competing description tags (this session's own v3.204.0 "ghost jobs" positioning fix was one of the two, never guaranteed to be the one a crawler actually reads), and worse, every one of the 6 real pages with no explicit `canonical` prop of their own (Pricing, Billing, DoNotSell, LegalIndex, and others) was silently inheriting the static tag's literal `href="https://ayn.careers/"` — telling Google their canonical version IS the homepage, a real de-indexing risk for pages that should canonicalize to themselves.

Fixed at the root cause, `SEO.tsx`, not in the six individual page files: a `useEffect` removes any `link[rel="canonical"]`/`meta[name="description"]`/`meta[name="robots"]` that does NOT carry Helmet's own `data-rh="true"` marker, once, the first time any page's real `<SEO>` mounts — index.html's static tags stay exactly as they were (still the correct pre-JS/no-JS fallback for a crawler or link-unfurler that never runs JavaScript), just no longer left standing once a real page takes over. The `robots` tag needed the identical fix for a sharper reason: a genuinely thin, `noIndex`-gated category page was found live still carrying the static `"index, follow"` tag right alongside its own correct `"noindex, nofollow"` one — the exact directive this whole feature exists to enforce, undermined by the same bug in a different tag.

A SECOND REAL BUG, FOUND CHASING WHY THE FIRST FIX APPEARED NOT TO WORK ON SOME ROUTES: `react-helmet-async` DEFERS ITS ACTUAL DOM COMMIT. `/jobs` and `/jobs/:id` both chain 2-3 separate fetch effects (search debounce, the list fetch, and for a direct `/jobs/:id` link, a second fetch for that one specific job) — enough back-to-back re-renders that Helmet's default `requestAnimationFrame`-batched DOM update kept getting rescheduled before it ever fired. Confirmed live with a temporary debug log: the component's own computed props (`pageTitle`, `canonicalUrl`, etc.) were correct on every single render, only the actual `<head>` tags never landed — ruling out a logic bug before touching anything. `<Helmet defer={false}>` is the library's own documented opt-out of that batching, committing synchronously on every render instead; the fix, not a workaround, and applied once in `SEO.tsx` so it covers all 16 call sites at once.

Verified live end to end against the real production database, not assumed from the code: a real, populated category (`/jobs/category/software_engineering`, genuine software-engineering-titled results), a real multi-word city (`/jobs/location/new-york-city`, correctly title-cased and matched), a deliberately nonexistent category (correctly empty state, correctly the sole `noindex, nofollow`), a real individual job (`/jobs/:id`, correct title/canonical/description and, this time correctly, `JobPosting` schema), the plain `/jobs` hub, `/pricing` (confirmed zero canonical tags, not a wrong one), and `/` itself (confirmed exactly one canonical, one description, and that description now actually saying "ghost job" with nothing competing against it). `npx tsc --noEmit`, eslint, and `npm run build` all clean.

Preceded by v3.204.0, the first real pieces of a research-backed positioning and design pass shipped: the seeker hero names "ghost jobs" directly, the headline reveals word by word instead of sitting static, and the three scattered trust-stat cards became one bento module with a live number.

TWO RESEARCH BRIEFS, THEN THE FIRST REAL SLICE OF BOTH BUILT. Asked directly, twice, to act as a positioning/SEO strategist and separately as a senior visual designer, both backed by real research rather than guessed at — two artifacts were produced first ("The Honest Board" on positioning, "The Design Audit" on layout), then asked to build what they recommended. This entry is the first, highest-confidence slice of both: the parts that were pure on-site code, no external tool (Search Console, PR outreach) required.

GHOST JOBS, NAMED, NOT JUST DESCRIBED. The positioning research's single strongest finding: 47-67% of job seekers report hitting a ghost job in 2026, and it's now a named, press-covered phenomenon (Axios ran a piece four days before this shipped) — a real, high-intent search term, not just a mechanism to explain. AYN's hero lead already gestured at the mechanism ("Company career pages only... never LinkedIn or Indeed") but never said the word. Now it does: "No ghost jobs. Company career pages only, refreshed continuously, never LinkedIn or Indeed." The site's own `<meta name="description">` had the same gap, plus a real, separate bug found while fixing it — it still read "AYN's AI reads the job posting off the page," leftover phrasing from the retired browser extension (the JSON-LD structured data got this exact fix at v3.202.0; this one plain tag was missed in that pass). Both now lead with the ghost-jobs hook inside the first ~155 characters most search snippets actually keep.

A HEADLINE THAT MOVES, NOT A BIGGER ONE. The design audit's cheapest, highest-visibility finding: AYN's type is bold but static, while 2026's own research names reactive, scroll/hover-responsive type as the year's defining typographic move. New `KineticHeadline.tsx` — same real headline text, same font, same size, words reveal in sequence on load and replay on hover, real `prefers-reduced-motion` support (renders every word already lit, no stagger, matching the site's existing IntersectionObserver-less fallback pattern). Takes plain `text`/`emphasis` strings rather than arbitrary JSX so it can reliably split into words — `HERO`'s type in `LandingSections.tsx` changed from a raw `JSX.Element` to `{headline: string; emphasis?: string}` to fit, covering both audiences, not just the seeker side the audit demoed it against.

THREE SCATTERED TRUST CARDS BECAME ONE BENTO MODULE, REUSING WHAT ALREADY EXISTED RATHER THAN FORKING IT. The design audit's other finding: freshness/sourcing numbers were repeated as flat, low-weight text across three separate places, never given real visual weight, and the page had zero bento-style layout anywhere. Turned out that second half wasn't quite right — a real `.lp-bento`/`.lp-tile`/`.lp-span-*` grid already exists and is already in real use further down the same page (the Features section's icon+title+description tiles) — corrected before building anything, rather than shipping a second, parallel bento system next to one that already works. New `TrustBento.tsx` reuses that exact grid, adding only content-shape modifier classes (`.is-stat`, `.lp-stat-k`/`.lp-stat-l`) for a big-number-plus-caption shape the existing tiles didn't have. Replaces the flat "Where jobs come from" strip for the seeker audience only (the employer strip has different, unrelated content and was left untouched). Five real tiles: a lead ember-gradient tile on the 3-day freshness window (the actual ghost-job answer, not a vanity metric), a live job count, the 2-hour refresh cadence, "zero invented facts," and "$0 to browse." The count is fetched live via the same anon-readable, scam-excluded `job_postings` query `/jobs` already uses — capped at the same "1,000+" convention `BrowseJobs.tsx` already established (v3.167.0: a precise five-digit figure on an unfiltered view reads as a vanity metric, not a feature), defaulting to that honest, always-true figure until the live fetch resolves, and staying there silently if it never does.

Verified live against the real dev server, not assumed from the code: DOM/computed-style inspection (this session's screenshot tool broke mid-verification, a genuine tooling failure unrelated to the change — confirmed by a fresh tab also failing identically at a bare page load with zero scroll involved) confirmed the kinetic headline's word-by-word reveal animation actually running (caught mid-animation twice), the bento module's real grid layout, gradient background, and exact tile text all correct, the employer audience correctly untouched (still the original flat strip, `bentoPresentForEmployer: false`), and no horizontal overflow at a 375px mobile width with the grid correctly collapsing to one column. `npx tsc --noEmit` and eslint both clean.

NOT YET DONE, FLAGGED HONESTLY: the bigger pieces of both briefs — programmatic SEO (category/location/salary pages), pitching AYN into "best job search site" roundups, a Browse Jobs density pass, breaking the landing page's repeated section rhythm further down the page — are real, larger follow-ups, not attempted in this pass.

Preceded by v3.203.0, Google Jobs structured data hardened against Google's own documented requirements, closing a gap flagged but never finished when Browse Jobs went public.

READ GOOGLE'S OWN DOCS DIRECTLY RATHER THAN GUESSING AT WHAT "JOBPOSTING SCHEMA" NEEDS. `/jobs/:id`'s `jsonLd` (`PublicJobs.tsx`, added at v3.201.0) had the basics — title, description, datePosted, hiringOrganization, jobLocation, employmentType, baseSalary — but was missing four fields Google's structured-data docs specifically call out, three of them tied to named, common real-world mistakes. `validThrough` (recommended, and Google's docs are explicit that omitting it makes a listing look permanently open) is now computed as `posted_at + 3 days` — not a guess, that's the literal real value of `job-board-sync`'s own `FRESHNESS_DAYS`, so this is the actual, honest moment a listing gets pruned from `job_postings` if it isn't reconfirmed live, not an invented expiry. `jobLocationType: 'TELECOMMUTE'` plus `applicantLocationRequirements` (US + CA, matching `job-board-sync`'s own real sourcing scope, not a per-job guess) now fires whenever `work_mode === 'remote'` — Google's docs name "remote job with no TELECOMMUTE flag" as the single most common JobPosting error, and this app's own data confirmed it: 2,137 of ~23,000 live postings are tagged remote and every one of them was only ever emitting a plain `jobLocation` before this fix. `directApply: false` is now explicit and accurate — AYN always sends an applicant to the employer's own site, never a fill-and-submit flow of its own, so this is a true fact stated plainly rather than an omission Google has to guess at. `identifier` (AYN's own internal id as a `PropertyValue`) was added too, cheap and harmless, helping Google track the same posting across re-crawls.

Verified live, not assumed from the code: rebuilt and ran the real production server locally against the real live database, loaded a real remote posting's `/jobs/:id` page, and read the actual rendered `application/ld+json` tag out of the DOM — confirmed `validThrough` landed exactly 3 days after `datePosted`, `jobLocationType`/`applicantLocationRequirements` both present and correct for the remote job, `directApply: false`, and a real `identifier`. `npx tsc --noEmit` clean.

Preceded by v3.202.0, the SEO cleanup that shipped alongside Browse Jobs going public but was left uncommitted and untested when that session ended.

TWO REAL GAPS, BOTH FROM THE SAME ROOT CAUSE: THE SITE'S OWN SEARCH-ENGINE-FACING DESCRIPTION STILL DESCRIBED A PRODUCT THAT NO LONGER EXISTS. `index.html`'s structured data (`SoftwareApplication` and `Organization` schema, read by Google to describe AYN in search results) still said "Job seekers install a Chrome extension that reads the job posting on the page" — accurate once, wrong since the extension was retired in v3.164.0 and Browse Jobs/`/check-resume` became the real free, public entry points. Rewritten to describe what AYN actually does today: real postings sourced from company career pages, free scoring and tailoring against a real resume, no invented facts.

THE OTHER GAP: ~23,000 REAL, INDIVIDUALLY-CRAWLABLE JOB PAGES THAT NO SITEMAP HAD EVER LISTED. `/check-resume` and the static `/jobs` listing page were added to `public/sitemap.xml`, but the real content — every individual `/jobs/:id` posting, the whole reason Browse Jobs went public in v3.201.0 (a real, per-job URL is what Google for Jobs' own `JobPosting` structured data needs to work at all) — had no sitemap entry anywhere. A static sitemap file can't hold this: the catalog changes constantly (a posting is pruned within 3 days of going stale). New `/sitemap-jobs.xml` route in `server.js`, generated live from the same public, scam-excluded anon read `/jobs` itself already uses (`job_postings_select_anon` RLS policy) — no new backend surface, no new risk, cached 10 minutes so a hot crawl can't hammer the database. Capped at 45,000 rows, a real margin under the sitemap protocol's actual 50,000-URL ceiling (today's real count: 23,375). `robots.txt` points crawlers at it alongside the existing sitemap.

A REAL BUG FOUND VERIFYING RATHER THAN TRUSTING THE UNCOMMITTED CODE: PostgREST caps rows per request at its own configured max (1,000 on this instance) regardless of what `limit` a request asks for — confirmed live, asking for all 45,000 in one call silently came back as exactly 1,000. The route already paged through with the `Range` header in 1,000-row batches, which was the right fix, just never actually run before this. Verified live end to end against the real production database: rebuilt, ran the real `server.js` locally, confirmed `/sitemap-jobs.xml` returns valid XML with 23,375 real `<url>` entries and a working 10-minute cache (a second request completed in 0.03s instead of the original 3.5s), and confirmed `/robots.txt` and `/sitemap.xml` both serve the new entries correctly.

`/CHECK-RESUME` HAD NO WAY TO BE FOUND. Confirmed live: the page itself loaded fine (`200`), but a full grep of the header, footer, and landing page found zero links pointing at it anywhere — the feature existed and worked, it just had no door in from the rest of the site. Closed by the same fix that answered the second half of the report (below): a "Browse jobs" nav link, plus a "Check my resume against a job" CTA card at the bottom of the new public jobs page, cross-linking the two free tools to each other.

BROWSE JOBS WAS NEVER ACTUALLY PUBLIC — CONFIRMED THE REAL SHAPE OF THE ASK BEFORE BUILDING RATHER THAN GUESSING. `job_postings` has only ever had one RLS policy, `authenticated`-only; Browse Jobs itself has always lived inside `/resume-hub`, gated behind sign-in, since v3.134.0. Asked directly whether "not public" meant the job listings themselves should be visible with no account (as opposed to just restating the check-resume gap) — confirmed yes.

New public route `/jobs` (list) and `/jobs/:id` (a specific posting, real per-job URL for sharing and for Google Jobs' own `JobPosting` structured data, which needs an individually crawlable URL to work at all — see the deferred SEO research this file's own history already flagged). `src/pages/PublicJobs.tsx` reads `job_postings` directly via the anon Supabase client, the identical pattern the authenticated `BrowseJobs.tsx` already uses (no new backend action needed — this is public content by nature, a company's own career-page posting, not user data, and the table holds zero PII). New RLS policy, `job_postings_select_anon`, grants `anon` SELECT — with the scam-exclusion filter baked into the policy itself (`scam_suspected IS NOT TRUE`), not just the client query, so an anonymous reader can never see a flagged listing regardless of what it asks for, the same defense-in-depth discipline this file's own v3.197.0 entry already established for the authenticated view.

Deliberately narrower than the authenticated board: no quick-match score (needs a resume, which an anonymous visitor doesn't have — the whole point of "sign up and score every job against your real resume" as the upsell), no swipe mode, no trending, no company-hiring badge or ranking. Search, browse, read the full posting, apply — the same honest floor a real public job board offers before asking for an account. Reused `BrowseJobs.tsx`'s own already-hardened helpers (`resolveSalary`'s tuned regex extraction, `resolveLogoUrl`'s ATS-vendor-host-aware favicon fallback, the deterministic `JobDescriptionBody` JD formatter) by exporting them rather than forking the logic — each of these had a real, documented bug-fix history of its own; duplicating them would have meant re-earning those fixes from scratch or silently drifting out of sync.

A REAL BUG FOUND LIVE, NOT BY READING THE CODE: exported every helper this new page needed except `postedDate` — missed on the first pass, and `npx tsc --noEmit` did NOT catch it (a genuine gap in this project's type-check coverage for a plain missing named export, worth remembering: a clean `tsc` here is not proof every import resolves). Caught immediately on the first live preview load — the whole page rendered `ErrorBoundary`'s fallback with `SyntaxError: The requested module ... does not provide an export named 'postedDate'`. Fixed with one more `export`, re-verified live.

`Header.tsx` gained a "Browse jobs" entry in the job-seeker nav list (`SEEKER_LINKS`), pointing at `/jobs` — deliberately not added to `EMPLOYER_LINKS`, since browsing job listings is a seeker-facing feature. Confirmed live in a real anonymous session (a genuine second confusion caught mid-verification, not a bug: the test browser's own `localStorage` still had the audience toggle set to "employer" from earlier testing in this same pass, so the nav correctly showed the employer link set with no "Browse jobs" entry at all — switching the toggle back to job-seeker and reloading showed it correctly).

Verified live end to end, not assumed from the code: a genuinely anonymous local preview loaded `/jobs` with zero session and rendered real listings (Ramp, Kestra Medical Technologies, and others); the search box correctly narrowed results to only the searched company's own postings; clicking a card correctly updated both the detail pane and the URL to `/jobs/:id`; a genuinely cold, direct load of that exact `/jobs/:id` URL (a fresh navigation, not a click-through) correctly resolved and rendered that one specific job, proving the deep-link fetch path works independent of the list already being loaded; "See how well I match, free" correctly opened the real sign-up modal. Deployed live via the real `auto_deploy.sh` path; confirmed the live `index-*.js` bundle hash matched the just-built one exactly, then re-verified directly against `https://ayn.careers`: both `/jobs` and a real `/jobs/:id` return `200`, and a real anon REST call (the exact request the live page itself makes) returned genuine, current job listings. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `node scripts/check-wiring.mjs` both clean.

Preceded by v3.200.0, a real, powerful public ATS keyword checker, built after a follow-up clarification: "there are two ways ats and ai to scan for the key words that filter the resume" meant two SCANNING METHODS on one page — a literal/ATS-style match (free) and an AI/semantic-style match (gated behind signup) — not two keyword categories. Explicit build instruction: "yes make sure it wont cost us money."

`/check-resume`, A REAL PUBLIC PAGE, ZERO NEW AI COST. No account needed to paste a resume and a job description and see exactly which requirements match by literal wording and which don't — the identical deterministic gap analysis (`computeGap`, `_shared/tailoring.ts`) every signed-in seeker's Score/Tailor flow already runs, zero AI call, zero embedding call, so an anonymous stranger running this a thousand times a day costs AYN nothing. New action `resume_check_public` sits in a small, explicit `PUBLIC_ACTIONS` allowlist checked BEFORE the mandatory-JWT check in `resume-hub/index.ts` — the one deliberate, hardcoded exception to "every action requires a session," mirroring the shape the retired extension PUBLIC LINK FLOW used for its own pre-auth actions, not a broad bypass. Anything ever added to that allowlist has to clear the same bar this one does by construction: no AI call, no outbound fetch, no write — deterministic text-in, deterministic-logic-out only. Both inputs capped at 20,000 characters server side, matching every other free-text input ceiling already used elsewhere in this file.

Reuses `buildSections(null, null, resumeText)` — already had a documented fallback for exactly this shape (no identity, no canonical profile, a bare pasted resume text) from its very first version, built for the "paste your text" degraded path, never exercised by a real caller with literally nothing but resume text until this feature. The AI-powered half — the one that would catch "led a team of 3" satisfying "team leadership experience" even though the words don't line up — is deliberately NOT free: the page states plainly that this is the literal match only, and offers a "See the deeper match, free" button that opens the real `AuthModal`, landing a genuinely interested person in the same credit-metered pipeline every other AI action in this product already runs through, not a new, ungated cost surface.

Verified live end to end, not assumed from the code: the real deployed edge function answered a genuine curl with zero `Authorization` header, correctly returning the deterministic match/missing/matchPct for a real bulleted JD (an earlier plain-prose test JD correctly returned nothing — a pre-existing, already-documented limitation of the deterministic bullet extractor, not a new bug). The real page was driven through a local dev preview with a genuinely anonymous, unauthenticated browser session: filled both fields, clicked Check my resume, confirmed the exact real result rendered on the live page (33% literal match, correct matched/missing lists, the AI-upsell card), then confirmed via a direct DOM query that clicking "See the deeper match, free" actually opens the real sign-up modal (`role="dialog"` content: "Welcome to AYN / Sign In / Sign Up / Continue with Google..."). `npx tsc --noEmit` clean, `node scripts/check-wiring.mjs` clean (`hub=16 actions all wired`). Deployed live via the real `auto_deploy.sh` path (not just the fast scp-and-restart path used during iteration) — confirmed the live `index-*.js` bundle hash matches the just-built one exactly, then re-ran both checks a second time directly against `https://ayn.careers`: the route returns a real `200`, and the live edge function, called fresh with zero auth header against the real production domain, returned the correct real result for a genuine test resume and JD.

Preceded by v3.199.0, the last of the three hiring-behavior pieces asked for in one thread: after "only show the badge when we're sure" (v3.198.0), asked plainly what the benefit of ranking would even be, then to build it.

RANKING, THE SAME ONE-DIRECTIONAL DISCIPLINE AS THE BADGE. New `company_hiring_status_batch(company_slugs)` RPC — Browse Jobs shows many distinct companies per page, and calling the single-company function once per row would mean dozens of round trips; this is one call for every distinct company already on the page, fetched once and cached, matching the shape of the existing per-page logo/company-activity lookups this file already documents. `BrowseJobs.tsx`'s existing Best match sort (`scores[id]` descending) now subtracts a fixed 20-point penalty from a job whose company carries a confirmed `showcase` verdict — and only `showcase`. `insufficient_data`/`uncertain` get no penalty at all, on purpose: per this file's own v3.195.0 entry, most companies sit in one of those two states for weeks, and treating "AYN doesn't have a confident read yet" the same as a real, earned negative would have quietly buried most of the actual catalog for no real reason. "Newest" mode is untouched — it's the page's own existing, explicit opt-out from any ranking logic at all, and staying out of it was a deliberate choice, not an oversight.

Verified live with a real, deliberately adversarial setup, not assumed from the code: seeded a synthetic company with real, qualifying `showcase` history, then gave it the single freshest `posted_at` job on the entire test page — under Best match with every job tied at a null score (a real throwaway account with no resume yet, so `job_board_score` legitimately returns null for everything), a plain recency tiebreak should have put the freshest job first. It landed at the very bottom of the page instead, directly in front of a genuine "Load more jobs" boundary — the only thing that could have moved it there is the penalty actually firing. The badge itself was independently reconfirmed absent for the same company in the same pass, so the two mechanisms don't conflict or double-count.

Preceded by v3.198.0, the active-hiring badge from v3.195.0's `company_hiring_status()` finally shown, and shown deliberately one-directional.

ASKED DIRECTLY: "only active hiring things we are not sure we leave it only badge with the companies we are sure about." `company_hiring_status()` (v3.195.0) had four possible outcomes; the badge only ever renders for exactly one of them. `BrowseJobs.tsx` gained a new effect matching the shape of its own existing `companyActivity` effect (fetched only for the currently open job, never the whole list — this is a real-time RPC call, not something to run per-row across a page of hundreds of jobs): calls `company_hiring_status(selected.company_slug)`, renders a `TrendingUp`-icon badge reading "{company} is actively hiring" directly under the page's existing "Sourced directly" trust line, but strictly only when the result is `active`. `showcase`, `uncertain`, and `insufficient_data` all render nothing — a negative-leaning label on a real, named company is a far bigger trust call than a positive one, and per this file's own v3.195.0 entry, `insufficient_data` is going to be the honest, correct answer for most companies for weeks regardless, so the badge stays genuinely rare rather than force a verdict the underlying data doesn't support yet.

Verified live, not assumed: seeded a synthetic company with real, qualifying `company_hiring_stats` history (20 tracked listings, 30% real turnover, 40 days observed), confirmed `company_hiring_status()` itself actually returned `active` for it before touching the UI at all, then drove a real throwaway account through the actual Browse Jobs page and confirmed the badge rendered — correct copy, correct icon, correct position, right below the existing trust line. The negative case was confirmed in the same pass for free: a real, already-present, unflagged company shown in the same session correctly rendered no badge at all, no extra test needed.

Preceded by v3.197.0, scam-listing detection made real: excluded from Browse Jobs once flagged, checked at ingestion (not days later), and a genuine false-positive near-miss caught and fixed before it ever mattered.

SINCE THE CHECKER ALREADY VISITS A LISTING'S REAL PAGE, ASKING ONE MORE QUESTION IN THE SAME CALL COSTS NOTHING. `job-checker/`'s prompt (v3.195.0's closure checker) now also asks whether the page shows real, explicit scam patterns — fee-up-front, sensitive info requested before a real interview, unrealistic pay — deliberately told not to flag a listing for being brief or vague alone. Verified both directions before it ever touched production: a real, legitimate listing correctly came back clean; a synthetic page built with explicit scam language (hosted temporarily on the internal Docker network, never public) correctly came back flagged, citing the exact red flags present. New `scam_suspected`/`scam_reason` columns on `job_postings`. Deliberately NOT auto-deleted the way a confirmed closure is — a scam verdict is a fuzzier judgment than "is there literally a closed banner on the page," so it's captured for visibility, nothing more, at least not yet.

REPORTED DIRECTLY, ONE MESSAGE LATER: "we cant show people scamed jobs." The signal was being recorded and never acted on — fixed by excluding `scam_suspected = true` from Browse Jobs' own query. The one real trap: most rows are still unchecked (`scam_suspected` is `null`), and a naive `.not("scam_suspected","eq",true)` would have silently dropped every one of those too, since SQL's three-valued logic treats `NOT (NULL = true)` as `NULL`, not `TRUE` — used an explicit `.or("scam_suspected.is.null,scam_suspected.eq.false")` instead. Verified against the real live REST API, not assumed: seeded a row explicitly flagged true, confirmed it was visible without the filter and confirmed excluded with the exact filter Browse Jobs now uses, and confirmed the filter composes correctly with the page's existing search/location `.or()` calls (PostgREST ANDs repeated `or=` params).

THE REAL GAP, CAUGHT BY THE FOUNDER, NOT FOUND INTERNALLY: "we cant have it scame before it even land browser." The AI-based checker only ever runs on candidates already 3+ days stale (or 14+ days by `created_at` via the spot-check) — a brand-new listing is fully visible in Browse Jobs the instant it's ingested, with zero check at all for days. Running the real headless-browser-plus-AI checker on every incoming job (up to ~2000/cycle) would be far too slow and costly. Fixed with a second, free layer instead: a deterministic keyword check (`_shared/scamSignals.ts`) against the description text already in memory at ingestion time — no extra fetch, no extra AI call — wired into both ingestion paths (`job-board-sync`, `ats-direct-sync`) so a listing can never reach a seeker even once unchecked, regardless of which function brought it in.

A REAL, LIVE FALSE-POSITIVE NEAR-MISS, FOUND AND FIXED BEFORE IT COULD ACTUALLY HIDE ANYTHING FROM A REAL USER. The first version matched bare vocabulary ("gift card", "western union") and, checked against real production data before being trusted (not assumed safe from the earlier synthetic tests alone), flagged three real, legitimate listings from well-known companies: a Twilio posting containing its own anti-fraud disclaimer ("we will never ask for payment, gift cards, cryptocurrency..." — the warning itself tripped the filter meant to catch what it was warning against), a Hy-Vee retail posting listing "Western Union" and a "money order machine" as real in-store equipment a Service Manager uses, and a third posting mentioning gift cards as an employee-of-the-week benefit. Rewritten around the lesson: every pattern now requires an actual request shape (a verb aimed at the applicant — "send", "pay", "provide your" — not bare vocabulary), plus a document-level negation check that skips the whole listing outright if it contains its own anti-scam warning anywhere. Verified against all three real false-positive texts (now correctly cleared) plus a stress-test set including a real payroll job describing bank-detail verification as a duty and a real bank-teller job describing wire transfers as a duty (both correctly stay clear, the exact failure class this rewrite targets) — 12/12 passed, plus a clean sweep of 2,000 more real, previously-unseen descriptions with zero further false positives.

A SECOND REAL BUG, FOUND CORRECTING THE FIRST ONE: THE STICKY-FLAG TRIGGER BLOCKED ITS OWN INTENDED ESCAPE HATCH. Since `job-board-sync` re-upserts the same ~2000 rows every 2 hours in one blanket call with no separate insert-vs-update code path, a `BEFORE UPDATE` trigger (`job_postings_scam_sticky`) makes a `true` flag sticky — once the deeper AI checker or the keyword pass confirms a listing, a later routine re-sync that finds nothing on its own shallower pass can never silently flip it back to `false`. Verified live: a seeded row with `scam_suspected: true` survived an explicit attempt to blank it back to `false`. But correcting the three real false positives moments later hit the exact same protection — the trigger can't tell a routine automated overwrite apart from a deliberate human correction, both being a plain true-to-false `UPDATE`. Fixed with a session-scoped bypass (`SET LOCAL ayn.allow_scam_downgrade = 'true'`) that only a deliberate manual correction sets; the automated ingestion functions never set it, so they stay exactly as protected as before. Verified both directions live: blocked without the bypass, succeeds with it.

Preceded by v3.195.0, a real closure checker plus company-level hiring-behavior tracking, built directly off the v3.194.0 conversation's own follow-up question: "won't tightening the freshness window make the job pool very little?"

INVESTIGATED THE ACTUAL QUESTION LIVE RATHER THAN REASONING FROM ASSUMPTION, AND THE REAL ANSWER TURNED THE REST OF THIS PASS. Checked whether narrowing `job-board-sync`'s `posted_within` window from 7 to 3 days actually shrinks what gets fetched — it doesn't: freehire's own API response explicitly lists `posted_within` under `meta.ignored_params`, confirmed live with two identical-except-for-that-param calls returning the exact same total (575,973). The real, load-bearing half of `FRESHNESS_DAYS` was always the *prune* cutoff, not the fetch filter — that half was never affected. Digging further into freehire's full (previously never-read) job object surfaced the real risk instead: `posted_at`, the one field AYN's whole prune/freshness system trusts, is not stable. Sampled the exact 2,000 jobs `job-board-sync` pulls every cycle: 45% are already classified `reality.class: "stale"` by freehire's own internal system, and 35% are genuinely 31-90+ days old by freehire's own `reality.age_days` — despite every one of them carrying a `posted_at` recent enough to rank in the "most recent" pull. Position ~2,000 (the very edge of what AYN fetches) had a `posted_at` only hours old on a listing freehire itself calls 24 days stale. freehire appears to keep bumping the field regardless of true age.

CHECKED WHETHER FREEHIRE'S OWN "STALE" LABEL WAS ITSELF TRUSTWORTHY BEFORE BUILDING ANYTHING ON IT, PER A DIRECT INSTRUCTION TO VERIFY RATHER THAN ASSUME. Sampled 8 "fresh" and 8 "stale" listings, fetched every real `apply_url` directly, and confirmed two of the most doubtful ones (a 63-day "stale" Apple listing, a 51-day "stale" OX Security listing) through a real rendered browser, not just raw HTML. All 16 were genuinely real, live, currently open positions — the Apple listing's own page read "Posted: Aug 21, 2026" (today), directly contradicting freehire's own 63-day figure. Conclusion: `reality.class`/`age_days` is not ground truth either, just a different, also-unreliable signal — `"stale"` means "freehire has tracked this listing a while," not "this job is fake or gone." Checked `closed_at` (never populated, 0/2,000) and `fake_freshness` (never true, 0/2,000) too — neither usable. The real, honest conclusion: freehire hands AYN no reliable "is this job still real" signal at all. The only trustworthy answer is to actually go look.

A REAL CLOSURE CHECKER, BUILT ON A SPECIFICALLY REQUESTED LIBRARY (ScrapeGraphAI), NOT AYN'S OWN AI-CALL PLUMBING. `job-checker/` (new, this repo): a small FastAPI service wrapping ScrapeGraphAI plus a real headless Chromium, visiting a listing's actual `apply_url` and asking whether it's genuinely still open. Runs as its own isolated, memory-capped Docker container — not part of `docker-compose.yml`, attached only to the internal `supabase_default` network with **no public port**, so it can never be reached from outside the VPS. It never holds the real AI credential itself: it calls through a new, minimal edge function, `ai-openai-bridge` (service-role-key-gated, internal-only), which is what actually reaches the same `AI_RELAY_URL`/`RELAY_SECRET` gateway `resume-hub`'s own `callAI()` already uses — the credential itself never leaves the properly-secured edge runtime. A real upstream bug was hit and fixed rather than downgraded around: ScrapeGraphAI 1.76.0's own code imports `ChatOllama` from a `langchain_community` re-export path the library's own stated `langchain_community>=0.4.0` requirement had already removed — fixed with a small compatibility shim aliasing the class back onto the old path (`langchain-ollama`, already an installed dependency, holds the same class), not a version downgrade.

WIRED INTO `job-board-sync` AS A REAL SAFETY NET, NOT A REPLACEMENT FOR THE EXISTING PRUNE. Before the blind elapsed-time delete runs, a small, wall-clock-budgeted batch of candidates gets a real check first: confirmed-open gets its `posted_at` honestly refreshed (a real, verified "still live" timestamp, unlike freehire's own gameable one) and survives; confirmed-closed gets deleted immediately, often faster than the 3-day wait; a check that errors or times out is left untouched, never deleted on an inconclusive result. Everything the batch doesn't reach still falls through to the exact same blind prune as before, so the "nothing survives past `FRESHNESS_DAYS` unverified forever" guarantee is never weakened, only ever strengthened for whatever a run can afford to check.

A REAL PRODUCTION TIMEOUT WAS HIT AND FIXED LIVE, NOT GUESSED AROUND IN ADVANCE. The first live run with the checker wired in was killed outright by the Edge Runtime's own worker supervisor (`WorkerRequestCancelled`) — the verify step's original 60-second wall-clock budget, stacked on top of the function's existing 2,000-job fetch+upsert work, ran past a real platform ceiling tighter than the 150s figure documented elsewhere in this file for other functions. Fixed by cutting the budget to 20s and the batch sizes down (15→8 main, 5→3 spot-check); re-verified live at ~33-56s total run time, comfortably inside the real ceiling. A second real bug surfaced by the same live-test discipline: a seeded row built specifically to prove the spot-check mechanism (see below) never got reached on the first successful run, because the larger main batch was processed first and consumed the whole wall-clock budget before the spot-check candidates got a turn — fixed by giving the small, purpose-built spot-check batch priority ordering, verified live afterward that the seeded row was reached and correctly checked.

COMPANY-LEVEL HIRING BEHAVIOR: "IS THIS COMPANY ACTUALLY HIRING, OR JUST KEEPING ROLES AS SHOWCASE," ASKED FOR DIRECTLY AFTER THE FRESHNESS DISCUSSION. A single listing's status is a snapshot; whether a *company* is genuinely hiring is a pattern over time — real turnover (postings actually get filled and replaced) versus the same listings sitting indefinitely. New table `company_hiring_stats` (migration `20260822010000_company_hiring_stats.sql`), maintained entirely by two triggers on `job_postings` so no application code has to remember to keep it in sync and so the aggregate survives even after the underlying listing rows themselves get pruned: an `AFTER INSERT` trigger increments a company's tracked-listings count (fires only for genuinely new rows — Postgres's own `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`, what a Supabase `.upsert()` compiles to, only fires the insert trigger on the no-conflict path, so a routine re-upsert of an already-known listing never double-counts); an `AFTER DELETE` trigger reads `OLD.closure_status` to tell a real, checker-confirmed closure apart from a plain blind prune (the checker's confirmed-closed path now writes `closure_status: "closed"` immediately before deleting, specifically so this trigger can see it). `company_hiring_status(slug)` classifies from plain, fixed arithmetic — never a model, never invented — into `insufficient_data` (fewer than 3 tracked listings, or under 14 days of observation — the honest default, not a guess), `active` (≥25% real turnover), `showcase` (<10% turnover after 30+ days watched), or `uncertain` (the honest middle ground neither threshold confidently supports).

THE FIRST TRIGGER'S NORMAL FIRING CONDITION WOULD NEVER CATCH THE EXACT COMPANY-BEHAVIOR PATTERN THIS WAS BUILT FOR — FOUND AND CLOSED BEFORE SHIPPING. The main closure-check batch only ever fires once AYN's own `posted_at` (copied straight from freehire's) goes stale — but freehire was just proven to keep that field looking recent for listings it privately knows are weeks old, meaning a company running a permanent "always hiring" listing that freehire keeps artificially refreshing would never age past `FRESHNESS_DAYS` and so would never reach the checker at all, no matter how long the listing genuinely sits. A second, independent candidate query was added specifically to close this: keyed on `created_at` (set once, on first insert, never touched by any later upsert — proven immune to freehire's own re-stamping) rather than `posted_at`, so a listing freehire keeps looking eternally fresh still gets a real, periodic, independent check. Verified live with a seeded row built to be invisible to the normal query (fresh `posted_at`, `created_at` backdated 20 days) — confirmed picked up and correctly checked only once the priority-ordering fix above landed.

VERIFIED THROUGHOUT WITHOUT WAITING REAL TIME TO PASS, THE SAME "PROVE IT, DON'T ASSUME IT" DISCIPLINE THIS FILE'S OWN HISTORY ALREADY DOCUMENTS REPEATEDLY. The trigger mechanics were proven with real inserts and deletes on a synthetic throwaway company (two real inserts confirmed `listings_tracked_total: 2`; a confirmed-closed delete confirmed `listings_confirmed_closed: 1`; a blind-prune delete confirmed `listings_blind_pruned: 1`, both counters correctly attributed). The classification thresholds were proven by seeding `company_hiring_stats` directly with fabricated historical data spanning all four real outcomes — 40 days observed at 5% turnover correctly returned `showcase`; the same company edited to 30% turnover correctly returned `active`; 15% (the honest middle) correctly returned `uncertain`; the same 30% turnover but only 5 days observed correctly returned `insufficient_data`, confirming the time-floor holds regardless of how much turnover has technically happened. All synthetic test data (a fake company, its `job_postings` rows, its `company_hiring_stats` row) was fully deleted afterward, confirmed against a final clean production-only run.

NOT YET DONE, HONESTLY: this whole company-hiring-behavior signal is backend-only right now — no badge on Browse Jobs, not yet used in ranking. It also can't produce a real, trustworthy verdict for any real company on day one by construction; `insufficient_data` is the correct, honest answer until enough real time has actually passed. A local macOS file-access permission broke mid-session (the app lost its Downloads-folder grant after a restart) and stayed broken for a significant stretch of this pass — everything above was built, live-tested, and verified directly on the VPS during that window rather than through this repo's normal git-tracked flow, then reconciled into a real commit once access was restored. `docker-compose.yml`'s `functions:` environment block needed a new `CHECKER_SECRET` line and `auto_deploy.sh` needed `ai-openai-bridge` added to its own explicit function list — both real, necessary infrastructure changes, and both live only on the VPS, never tracked in this repo, exactly like every other compose/deploy-script change this file's history already documents. See docs/map/deployment.md for the concrete gotcha this pass surfaced: a plain `docker restart` does not pick up a new or changed secret, only a full `docker compose up -d --force-recreate` does — confirmed live the hard way, two full restart cycles in, before the real cause was found.

Preceded by v3.194.0, the job-posting freshness/prune window tightened from 7 days to 3, asked for directly after discussing how a listing an employer pulled early could still sit in AYN's own data for up to a week.

WHY 3, NOT SOMETHING SHORTER OR SOMETHING SMARTER. The founder asked two related questions in sequence: whether AYN's ATS coverage includes every real company (investigated live — RBC runs Workday, which `ats-direct-sync` doesn't cover, a real and disclosed gap, not fixed this pass), and whether a listing pulled early could be removed from AYN faster than the standing 7-day window. `job-board-sync`'s prune has always been a pure elapsed-time cutoff against `posted_at` ("last confirmed live," re-stamped every sync cycle a posting is still returned by the source, never the original publish date) — not tied to how fast the disappearance is actually detected, which every 2-hour sync cycle already does almost immediately. A consecutive-missed-syncs based removal (react to detection, not just elapsed time) and a hiring-velocity signal built on `created_at` (which genuinely persists across repeated upserts, confirmed empirically, unlike `posted_at`) were both raised as possible follow-ups but explicitly not built — the founder's own instruction was narrower and direct: "okay we make it 3 days not seven days." `FRESHNESS_DAYS` (`job-board-sync/index.ts`) is the single constant driving both freehire's own `posted_within` query param and the shared prune cutoff (`delete().lt("posted_at", cutoff)`, no source filter, so it also governs rows written by `ats-direct-sync`) — changed from `7` to `3` in one place, so sourcing and pruning stay exactly as tight as each other, same as before. Every "7 days"/"7-day" reference in `BrowseJobs.tsx` (five spots: the file's own header comment, the posted-date-formatting comment, the company-activity feature's two comments, and — the one genuinely user-facing spot — the "sourced directly" trust-signal tooltip's title text) updated to match, so nothing in the UI still claims a window the backend no longer runs.

Verified live, not assumed from the diff: deployed via the real `auto_deploy.sh` path, confirmed the deployed function's own source inside the `supabase-edge-functions` container reads `FRESHNESS_DAYS = 3`, then invoked `job-board-sync` for real with the actual service-role key — `{"ok":true,"fetched":2000,"upserted":1916,"pruned":6959,...}`, a real prune of 6,959 rows a 7-day cutoff would have kept. A direct query immediately after confirmed the oldest surviving row was exactly `2.999...` days old, not "close to 3" — the cutoff holds precisely, not approximately.

Preceded by v3.193.0, a self-review of v3.192.0's own fix, run in the persona of a senior design/functional QA engineer at the founder's request. Found and fixed a real state-management race, then — while verifying the polish items from that same review — found and fixed a real CSS layout bug and a real effect-timing bug the first attempted fix at it didn't actually solve, plus a temporal-dead-zone hazard introduced while fixing that.

CRITICAL, FOUND BEFORE SHIPPING: `EmployerHub.tsx` tracked `userId` and `session` as two independently-resolving promises (`getUser()` and `getSession()`) with no loading gate blocking the shell, unlike `ResumeHub.tsx`'s own `if (loading) return`. A fast click on the new Settings tab during that window could render `SettingsPanel` with `session` already resolved (passing its own `!session` guard) but `userId` still `null`, silently cast to `string` via `userId!` — every query inside the four settings sections would then run against `null`, breaking silently with no error shown. Fixed by removing the separate `userId` state entirely and reading `session.user.id` instead, collapsing two racing promises into one; the Settings tab now shows a real loading state until `session` resolves rather than a state that could look ready but wasn't.

THREE MORE FOUND WHILE VERIFYING THE REVIEW'S OWN "SHOULD FIX" ITEMS LIVE, NOT ASSUMING THE FIRST PASS AT EACH WAS CORRECT. One, `SettingsPanel.tsx`'s Account/Notifications/Privacy/Sessions switcher had no tab semantics at all — real `role="tablist"/"tab"/"tabpanel"`, `aria-selected`, `aria-controls`, and roving-tabindex arrow-key navigation added (adding `role="tab"` without the keyboard behavior ARIA authoring practices expect would have been a worse mismatch than not using the role at all). Two, the same panel's section state reset to "account" every time the parent's conditional render unmounted it — persisted to `sessionStorage` instead, read as the lazy initial state, matching `ResumeHub.tsx`'s own top-level tab persistence pattern.

Three — the real find of this pass. A mobile-viewport check (found incidentally, pre-existing, not caused by v3.192.0) showed the rail's active item clipped at the edge of its own horizontal-scroll row. First attempt: a `scrollIntoView` effect keyed on `[tab]`. Live-verified it directly via `getComputedStyle`/manual `scrollIntoView()` calls and found it did nothing — `scrollLeft` stayed `0` even called by hand. Root cause was CSS, not JS: `.rh-navlist` (a flex child of `.rh-aside-left`, the real `overflow-x: auto` scroll container on mobile) had no `flex-shrink` set, so flexbox's default shrink behavior compressed it below its own content's natural width — `.rh-aside-left`'s `scrollWidth` never grew enough to make the last item reachable by scrolling at all, a content-width bug upstream of scroll position. Fixed with `flex-shrink: 0` on `.rh-navlist`/`.rh-navitem` plus `width: max-content` on the list. Re-verified live afterward and found the `scrollIntoView` effect itself *still* didn't fire on the one path that mattered most — a returning user whose stored tab is already `"home"` on mount: the effect's first run happened while the real rail was still behind the `loading` gate (`navListRef.current` null), and since `tab` never changed value again afterward, it never got a second chance. Fixed by adding `loading` (`orgLoading`/`profileComplete` on the employer side) to the dependency array. Wiring that into `EmployerHub.tsx` at its original declaration site would have referenced `profileComplete` before its own `const` — a real temporal-dead-zone `ReferenceError`, the same class of bug this file's own history already documents once (`BrowseJobs.tsx`'s `filtersOpen`, v3.169.0) — caught before shipping by tracing the actual line order, not assumed safe from `tsc` (which doesn't catch TDZ issues) or eslint (which didn't flag it either). Fixed by moving the effect to after `profileComplete`'s declaration instead.

Verified live end to end, in stages, redeploying between each real fix rather than batching: a fresh employer account through the exact `userId`/`session` race scenario (clean, no broken state); a fresh employer account through both the `profileComplete: false` and `profileComplete: true` render paths with zero console errors at either stage, confirming the TDZ fix actually holds at runtime, which `tsc`/eslint cannot verify on their own; the ARIA attributes confirmed correct via direct DOM inspection (`role="tab"`, `aria-selected`, roving `tabIndex`); the settings-section persistence confirmed by switching away and back; and, on the real production site with `sessionStorage` deliberately seeded to reproduce the exact "returning user lands on Home" case that was broken before, the rail's active item confirmed rendering in full, correctly auto-scrolled into view. `npx tsc --noEmit` and eslint clean throughout (two pre-existing, unrelated warnings only). Four throwaway accounts created and fully erased across this pass via `self_delete_account`.

Preceded by v3.192.0, Settings stopped being a separate page. Reported directly, live: "I see the website have two different design two different type of buttons... I dont go a different page."

FOUND BY ACTUALLY SIGNING IN AS BOTH ROLES AND READING THE COMPUTED STYLES, NOT GUESSING. Investigated with a real throwaway seeker account and a real throwaway employer account, both injected via a real session (not simulated), before touching any code. The seeker side turned out to already be fine: Resume Hub's "Home" nav item has rendered Settings inline since a much earlier pass (`v3.174.0`, `HomeTab.tsx`) — this session's own first read of that file, from earlier in the conversation, was simply wrong, a stale/misremembered description that never matched the real code; re-reading it fresh confirmed it already embeds the four settings sections (Account, Notifications, Privacy, Sessions) with no route change. The actual bug was employer-only: `EmployerHub.tsx`'s own "Settings" rail item still called `navigate("/settings")`, landing on the standalone `Settings.tsx`/`SettingsLayout.tsx` route — which was never brought inside `.resume-hub-theme` at all, running instead on its own separate `.settings-surface` scope that only ever retinted `--primary`, never the canvas background or typography. Confirmed via direct `getComputedStyle` calls on the live site: that route's background was plain white (`#ffffff`, not the real `#fbf6f0` warm paper), its font was `Inter` (the generic system default, not Outfit/Figtree), and its active tab was a flat solid fill with `background-image: none` — no gradient at all. Grepped the whole frontend for `/settings` first: the seeker side (`ResumeHub.tsx`) had zero references to it, meaning a real seeker could never have reached the broken page through the UI at all; the employer side's own rail button was the only surviving in-app link.

FIXED BY SHARING WHAT ALREADY WORKED, NOT BUILDING A SECOND IMPLEMENTATION. `HomeTab.tsx`'s content (the four-section settings switcher, already correctly reading `--rh-*` tokens with a `hsl(var(--x))` fallback in every one of the underlying `src/components/settings/*` components) moved out to a new `src/components/shared/SettingsPanel.tsx`, taking the same `{userId, session}` props. `ResumeHub.tsx`'s lazy import and its "home" tab's render line were updated to point at the new shared component; nothing else about the seeker side changed. `EmployerHub.tsx` gained real `userId`/`session` state (it had only ever tracked `userEmail`/`userName` before, fetched via `getUser()` with no matching `getSession()` call), `"settings"` was added as a real `EmployerTab` value, the rail button's `onClick` changed from `navigate("/settings")` to `setTab("settings")` with the same `active` styling every other rail item gets, and the page's own generic heading row (driven by `EMPLOYER_NAV.find(...)`.label/hint) is skipped specifically for the settings tab since `SettingsPanel` carries its own heading — avoiding a double "Settings" title rather than adding a second heading mechanism. The standalone `/settings` route itself was left untouched, not deleted: with both surfaces now settings-inline, nothing in the app links to it any more, but deleting a still-existing, still-working route was outside what was asked and outside what needed touching to fix the actual report.

Verified live end to end with two fresh throwaway accounts (one seeker, one employer with a completed company profile, both created and destroyed this session): clicking Settings on either surface confirmed via `location.href` to never leave the page, `getComputedStyle` confirmed `Outfit, Figtree` typography and the real ember gradient on the active tab in both cases, and the Account/Notifications/Privacy/Sessions sub-tabs all switched correctly with real data (the employer's own "Verify Co" company name, real plan/usage numbers) rendered in place. `npx tsc --noEmit` and eslint both clean (the two pre-existing warnings on unrelated `useEffect` dependency arrays, unchanged). `npm run build` clean. Deployed live via the VPS's own `auto_deploy.sh`. Both test accounts fully erased after via `self_delete_account`.

Preceded by v3.191.0, every remaining flat-colored primary button across the employer surface brought onto the same solid ember-gradient treatment the seeker side's primary buttons already use — reported directly against a screenshot of "Find candidates" / "Start over" looking off.

THE PILL FIX ONE VERSION AGO WAS ONE SPOT; THE SAME GAP WAS ACROSS SEVEN MORE BUTTONS. `v3.190.0` fixed the topbar usage pill's own style, but the report that followed ("still all the buttons in the app still different for what we have for the candidate") pointed at a wider, systemic gap: `.resume-hub-theme button.bg-foreground` (`resume-hub.css`) gives every plain shadcn `Button` with no explicit style a flat solid `--rh-accent` fill — that CSS-level fallback is what the seeker side's own primary CTAs (Continue, Optimize, Tailor, Send proposal, and every other one, checked via grep across `resume-hub/*.tsx`) all deliberately override with an inline `style={{ background: "var(--rh-gradient)", boxShadow: "var(--rh-glow)" }}`, one button at a time, over many versions — but the same per-button upgrade had never been done anywhere in the employer components, so every one of them was still quietly falling through to the flat fallback the seeker side had already moved past. Checked every `<Button>` across all 7 employer component files (`IntakeWizard.tsx`, `CandidateResultCard.tsx`, `CandidateAskCards.tsx`, `CompanyProfile.tsx`, `AssessmentDialog.tsx`, `AssessmentsPanel.tsx`, `EmployerHub.tsx`) to separate real primary CTAs from ghost/outline/icon buttons (which already correctly match the seeker side's own unstyled secondary buttons — confirmed by grep that seeker-side `variant="outline"` buttons also carry no inline style, so "Start over"'s pale outline was never the mismatch). Found and fixed 7 primary buttons still on the flat fallback: `IntakeWizard.tsx`'s opening-screen "Continue," the per-step "Continue," "Find candidates" (the one in the report), and the free-text "Save"; `AssessmentDialog.tsx`'s "Send assessment"; and `EmployerHub.tsx`'s two "Send a job proposal" / "Send proposal" dialog buttons. `CandidateResultCard.tsx`'s own "Send a job proposal" already had the gradient (from the original v3.12.0 pass) and needed no change.

Verified live with a fresh throwaway employer account, driven through the entire 8-step intake to the summary screen: every fixed button's computed style confirmed via direct DOM inspection (`getComputedStyle`) to carry the real `linear-gradient(135deg, #e85d3a 0%, #ff8a5c 100%)` background and the ember glow `box-shadow`, not the flat fallback — including "Find candidates" itself, the exact button from the report. `npx tsc --noEmit` clean. Test account and its seeded org fully erased after via `self_delete_account`.

Preceded by v3.190.0, the employer usage pill (searches/proposals/assessments in the topbar) finally matched to the seeker credit pill's real style, reported directly against a screenshot.

THE PILL'S OWN CODE COMMENT SAID IT WAS ALREADY DONE; IT WASN'T. `EmployerHub.tsx`'s topbar usage pill (`0/25 searches • 0/5 proposals • 0/3 assessments`) carried a v3.181.0 comment claiming it was "restyled as a real ember stat, same treatment as the seeker credit pill" — but the actual style underneath was still the old outline+tint chip (`border: 1px solid var(--rh-accent)`, `background: var(--rh-tint)`), never the solid `var(--rh-gradient)` + `var(--rh-glow)` treatment the seeker credit pill (`ResumeHub.tsx`) and every primary CTA on this page already use. The comment described an intent that never actually shipped. Fixed by copying the seeker pill's real style exactly: solid ember gradient background, white text, glow shadow, a `Zap` icon, with the divider dots switched from `var(--rh-accent)` to `bg-white/60` so they stay visible against the now-solid background instead of disappearing.

Verified live with a fresh throwaway employer account (approved, org completed) driven through to the Search tab: the pill renders as a solid ember gradient with the Zap icon and glow shadow, pixel-matching the seeker side. `npx tsc --noEmit` clean. Test account and its seeded org fully erased after via `self_delete_account`.

Preceded by v3.189.0, two fixes reported directly against a live screenshot of the employer surface: Settings moved into the nav rail, and the Search wizard's still-un-tokened colors brought onto the ember system.

SETTINGS WAS THE ONE THING ON THIS PAGE NOT LIVING IN THE RAIL. `EmployerHub.tsx`'s topbar had a standalone gear icon next to Sign out, calling `navigate("/settings")` — everything else (Search, Proposals, Assessments, Company) lived in the nav rail as a real, labeled row. Moved Settings into the rail as its own `.rh-navitem`, last in the list (matching the seeker side's own "Home" convention), same styling, same route underneath — a placement fix, not a new page or a new tab-switching state.

THE SEARCH WIZARD WAS STILL ON PLAIN SHADCN DEFAULTS UNDERNEATH THE STEP MAP. `IntakeWizard.tsx`'s completed-step pills used flat `--rh-hair`/`--rh-muted` grey with no ember at all once a step was no longer the active one, and the "role AYN will search for" summary rows (border, divider lines, the edit-pencil icon) were still on raw `border-border`/`text-muted-foreground` Tailwind defaults — never migrated onto the `--rh-*` tokens the rest of this file already uses in the spots built at a different time. A completed step now gets a quiet ember tint (distinct from the bold active state, but still branded, not default grey); the summary rows and their pencil icons now use `--rh-hair`/`--rh-muted`/`--rh-ink`/`--rh-accent-2` like everywhere else.

Verified live with a real throwaway employer account, driven all the way through the eight-step intake to the summary screen: Settings renders correctly in the rail on every tab, and the pencil icons and row text are visibly ember/warm-toned instead of grey. `npx tsc --noEmit` and eslint both clean, no new warnings. Test account and its seeded org fully cleaned up after (the org went with it automatically via the erasure cascade).

Preceded by v3.188.0, the rail's top mark now shows the real signed-in person's own initial instead of a hardcoded "A."

REPORTED DIRECTLY AGAINST A SCREENSHOT, RIGHT AFTER THE RAIL REDESIGN SHIPPED. The rounded-square, single-letter, ember-filled mark at the top of the nav rail reads exactly like a personal avatar (that's the standard shape apps use for one) — but it was a hardcoded "A" for "AYN," the same letter for every signed-in person regardless of who they actually are. `initialFromIdentity(name, email)` (name first, since that's what a person actually recognizes as themselves; email as the fallback for an account with no name on file; "A" only survives as the fallback-of-last-resort) now drives it in both `ResumeHub.tsx` (already had a `session` in scope) and `EmployerHub.tsx` (which didn't track the signed-in user at all before this — added a small `supabase.auth.getUser()` fetch on mount). The element's `aria-hidden` was removed too, since it's no longer decorative brand chrome — it now conveys real identity, so it gets a real `aria-label`/`title` instead.

Verified live: a real throwaway account signed up as "Zara Test" (deliberately a different first letter than its own `@example.com` test address, to prove the fix reads the real name rather than coincidentally still falling through to something else) showed a real "Z" mark, not "A". `npx tsc --noEmit` and eslint both clean, no new warnings. Test account erased after.

Preceded by v3.187.0, the shared icon rail (`.rh-aside-left`/`.rh-navlist`/`.rh-navitem`, used verbatim by both `ResumeHub.tsx` and `EmployerHub.tsx` since v3.181.0) redesigned after a direct ask to make it something "the new gen will love," backed by real research rather than a guessed-at reskin.

RESEARCHED FIRST, THEN BUILT THE ONE OPTION THE FOUNDER PICKED. Three live web-research passes: 2026 sidebar/nav UX trends, icon-only-nav accessibility specifically, and what Gen Z actually responds to in app UI. The one finding that mattered most wasn't a style preference, it was a named, repeated UX defect: icon-only navigation with a label that only appears on hover doesn't work at all on touch (there is no hover state on a phone), and every source that covered sidebars this round converged on the same fix — a real, always-visible label next to the icon, not a tooltip. Second finding: Gen Z UX research (and Google's own Material 3 Expressive, cited at 70% user preference in its own testing) points at "functional maximalism" — bold color, motion, real personality — over sterile minimalism, but the right read for AYN specifically is pushing its own already-established ember gradient harder, not importing a generic maximalist look that would fight the deliberate Charcoal & Ember identity. Presented both findings plus three build options (labels + bolder active state / keep icon-only but fix accessibility underneath / a fuller motion-heavy redesign); the founder picked the first.

WHAT ACTUALLY CHANGED. The rail widened from a fixed 64px icon-only column to 208px so a real label fits beside every icon (`rh-navlabel`, always rendered, not conditional on hover) — the old `.rh-tip` hover-tooltip span is deleted outright, not just hidden, since a permanent label makes a tooltip repeating the same text pure dead weight. The active item now fills with the real `var(--rh-gradient)` ember gradient plus its glow shadow — the same treatment every primary button in the app already uses — replacing a flat pink tint plus a thin left accent bar; the bar is gone too, since a fully gradient-filled pill is already a strong enough signal that a second indicator next to it read as redundant. A small `:active { transform: scale(0.97) }` press effect was added, a cheap, real piece of the "physics-based motion" research finding. On mobile, the horizontal icon row keeps its labels too (icon above, small label below, still scrollable) rather than reverting to icon-only — a bare icon row on the exact surface phones actually use it on would have reopened the same discoverability gap this whole pass exists to close. The notification badge (real, honest — only shows on genuine new proposals/assessments, never fake urgency) moved from an absolute-positioned dot to an inline chip pushed to the row's right edge on desktop, and back to a small corner dot on mobile's tighter stacked layout; it turns translucent white instead of solid ember when the item is active, so it doesn't disappear into the gradient fill.
