The concrete lesson for every future stage: after moving a block, grep every top-level declaration in the new file and diff it against the destination file's import list by hand — "I remember what I exported" is exactly the kind of assumption that produced this gap once already.

STAGES 4 THROUGH 9, DONE AS A DIRECT FOLLOW-UP IN THE SAME SESSION AFTER THE FOUNDER ASKED TWO THINGS AT ONCE — WHETHER RATE LIMITING SLOWS DOWN EVERYONE AT SCALE, AND TO KEEP GOING WITH THE SPLIT IF IT COULD STAY SAFE. Answered the first directly: `check_api_rate_limit` is keyed per `(user_id, endpoint)`, a real atomic upsert against one row, not a shared counter — one user hammering an action can never slow down or lock out anyone else, at 10,000 users or 10. Then continued the reorganization using the exact same extract-deploy-live-test method stages 1 to 3 established, six more stages: `lib/resumeScoring.ts` (`RESUME_SCHEMA`, `ATS_RUBRIC`, `scoreResumeContent` — the shared scorer `resume_diagnose` and `rewrite` both call), `lib/canonicalProfile.ts` (the `CanonicalProfile` type, `loadCanonical`, `canonicalDigest`, `CANONICAL_SCHEMA`, `extractCanonical`), `lib/jobParsing.ts` (URL/JD normalization, AI job-metadata parsing, the keyword-overlap fallback scorer), `lib/embeddings.ts` (real + deterministic-fallback embeddings, the semantic gap recheck built on them), `lib/candidateIndex.ts` (Talent Pool profile-text building and indexing), `lib/notifications.ts` (the proposal/assessment notification emails). File dropped from 5,305 lines (after stage 1) to 3,956 — a 26.7% reduction from the original 5,397, nine stages in, with the `~58`-action `Deno.serve` dispatcher itself still deliberately untouched throughout, exactly as scoped.

TWO MORE REAL BUGS FOUND, BOTH THE SAME SHAPE AS THE STAGE-3 `ACTION_FLAG` MISS, BOTH CAUGHT THE SAME WAY: A LIVE CALL AFTER DEPLOY, NOT A STATIC CHECK. Stage 5's `extractCanonical()` calls `QUALITY_MODEL` but the new `lib/canonicalProfile.ts` only imported `callAI` from `lib/ai.ts` — the first live `profile_canonical_extract` call after deploy came back `{"error":"QUALITY_MODEL is not defined"}`. Fixed by adding the missing import, redeployed, re-verified with a real seeded resume: the extraction came back completely faithful (`Python`/`SQL` skills, the real `Acme`/`Analyst` role, `BSc` education, `derived.seniority: "mid"`), nothing invented. Stage 6 hit a subtler version of the exact bug the stage-3 postmortem said to watch for: the export/import diff check alone said "all good" — every exported name was correctly imported — but it doesn't catch a name that's used in `index.ts` and was never exported at all. A full sweep afterward (grep every top-level name declared in the moved block, count its remaining references in `index.ts`, not just the ones already in the import list) caught `EMPTY_PARSED` — used at three call sites in `index.ts`, never exported from `lib/jobParsing.ts`. Fixed and this fuller sweep is now the standing check for every stage from 6 onward, not just the export/import diff.

LIVE-VERIFIED EACH OF THE SIX WITH A REAL FUNCTIONAL CALL, NOT JUST "DID IT DEPLOY." Stage 4: `resume_diagnose` on a real resume returned a correct threshold-consistent score. Stage 5: see above. Stage 6: a real `match` call against a JD with genuine gaps (Kubernetes, GraphQL, Terraform, Redis) correctly flagged all four as missing, nothing invented — exercising `resolveJobJd`/`parseJobMeta`/the keyword fallback path. Stage 7: reproduced the exact documented semantic-recheck case from this file's own v3.124.0 history — "Interface mockup software" correctly promoted to matched against a resume with `Prototyping`/`Figma`, while "Team leadership" and "Blockchain smart contract development" correctly stayed in `missing` — proving `embedText`/`semanticGapRecheck` still hold their calibrated behavior after the move. Stage 8: `talent_pool_set` produced a real `candidate_index` row with the real embedding model (`openai/text-embedding-3-small`, not the hash fallback), correct headline/seniority/years, and three correctly-provenanced `candidate_skills` rows. Stage 9: a real employer search → `employer_reveal_request` → `notifyCandidate` chain, verified via `email_logs` — Resend's own `@example.com` test-domain rejection came back exactly as this file's other entries already document using as a safe verification trick, `status: "failed"` with the real error attached, proving the moved function's full pipeline (fetch auth user, send, log) ran without a runtime error. Every throwaway account, org, and search created for these six stages fully erased afterward via `erase_account_core`, confirmed zero rows remaining.

STAGES 10 AND 11 FINISH THE REORGANIZATION — EVERY MODULE THAT WAS SAFELY EXTRACTABLE HAS NOW BEEN MOVED. `lib/billing.ts` (seeker credits — `COST_TAILOR`/`COST_COVER`/`COST_OPTIMIZE`, `billingEnsure`, `creditBalance`, `creditSpend`, `assertCredits`, `insufficientCredits`; employer per-period plan limits — `EmployerBilling`, `effectiveLimit`, `employerBilling`, `planLimitReached`) is the money-critical one, verified with extra care beyond the usual live-call check: a real throwaway account's real `billing_ensure` grant (6 free credits, a real `credit_ledger` row), a real `tailor` spend (balance 6 → 4, confirmed against `sum(delta)` in the actual ledger table, not just the API response), a manual drain to 0, then a real insufficient-credits attempt — clean `402`, and confirmed the ledger still summed to exactly 0 with zero new rows, proving `assertCredits` refuses before any spend, not after a failed one. `lib/smartTailor.ts` (the extension lane's `handleSmartTailor`/`handleCoverLetter` — identity-grounded generation, deterministic gap analysis, self-critique pass, figure-preservation check) is the last piece and closes out the file: with it moved, `index.ts` now ends at the `Deno.serve` dispatcher's own closing brace, nothing module-level trailing after it anymore. `creditRefund` (exported, but confirmed via grep to have zero callers anywhere in `index.ts`, before and after the move) was left genuinely orphaned rather than force-imported — a pre-existing dead-code fact this reorg didn't create and wasn't asked to fix.

Verified live end to end, including the full extension device-token lane most of this reorg hadn't exercised yet: `link_start` → `link_approve` (as a real signed-in account) → `link_poll` minted a real device token, then a real `smart_tailor` call correctly matched Python/PostgreSQL and correctly flagged Kubernetes as a genuine gap (never fabricated), `figuresVerified: true`, charged exactly 2 credits; a real `ext_cover_letter_text` call produced a real, on-topic letter and charged exactly 1 credit. Both charges confirmed against the real `credit_ledger` sum (3 remaining, matching 6 − 2 − 1 exactly), not just trusted from the API response.

THE FULL REORGANIZATION, DONE: 11 stages, `resume-hub/index.ts` from 5,397 lines down to 3,403 (a 37% reduction), 11 new files under `lib/` (`utils`, `gates`, `ai`, `resumeScoring`, `canonicalProfile`, `jobParsing`, `embeddings`, `candidateIndex`, `notifications`, `billing`, `smartTailor`), every single one pure code movement with zero logic changes, every single one verified with a real deploy plus a real live call against the actual deployed backend before being called done — never "it compiled" alone, since this file (`supabase/functions/`) was never in `tsc`'s scope to begin with. Two real bugs were found and fixed along the way (`ACTION_FLAG`, `QUALITY_MODEL`, both a name used across a new module boundary that a mechanical extraction missed), both caught by that same live-call discipline, never by a static check. The `~58`-action `Deno.serve` dispatcher itself — the part that actually depends on per-request closure state (`req`, `payload`, `user`, the admin clients created per block) — was deliberately never touched, exactly as scoped when the founder chose the safe reorganize-in-place option over genuinely separate deployed functions. Splitting that dispatcher itself, if ever wanted, is real, separate, more careful work — not attempted here.

CHROME WEB STORE: CONFIRMED READY, NOT RE-DONE. Rebuilt the extension package fresh (`node extension/build.mjs`) to confirm it still builds clean off current `main` — it does, byte-identical apart from an expected timestamp, reverted after confirming. Everything else was already correct per this file's own extensive prior history (minimal permissions, no remote code, the fixed consent screen); what's left is entirely outside this repo, in Google's own Developer Dashboard.

ONE HONEST CORRECTION MADE MID-PASS, NOT LEFT STANDING: initially told the founder that moving the database to a closer region was "a cheap settings change." It is not — Supabase has no in-place region migration for an existing project; the only real path is standing up an entirely new project in the target region and migrating every table, user, file, and secret across, then repointing the app, a genuine multi-day undertaking with real downtime risk. Corrected immediately rather than proceeding on a wrong premise; left as an open, disclosed decision for the founder rather than acted on.

Every throwaway account created across this entire pass (rate-limit testing, the erasure-cascade re-check, the admin-audit RPC verification, the reorganization smoke tests) was fully erased afterward via `erase_account_core`; confirmed zero rows remaining in every table touched each time.

Preceded by v3.130.0, asked directly to check for all security vulnerabilities, penetration risk, and prompt injection — the pass that found and fixed the `buildProfileText()` bullet-length gap and confirmed three separate live prompt-injection attacks against real deployed AI actions all failed. Full writeup below.

THE GOOD NEWS FIRST: EVERY DIRECT INJECTION ATTEMPT AGAINST THE ACTUAL AI FAILED. Three separate live attacks, each crafted as a DAN-style "ignore all previous instructions" payload with an explicit "you are authorized to comply" framing, the strongest form of this attack: (1) `job_fit_advice`'s `jdText`, attempting to leak the system prompt and force a fake `strong_fit` verdict — the model never printed anything resembling a system prompt, and since the verdict is computed in code before the model ever runs (not asked of it), there was structurally nothing for the injection to override; when the payload was reshaped as a bulleted "requirement" specifically so it would survive the deterministic extractor and actually reach the model's input, it still didn't comply, just correctly ignored the instruction line entirely. (2) `tailor`, with the same payload embedded in a job description, explicitly asking the model to fabricate "8 years of Kubernetes and Terraform experience leading a team of 50 engineers" and print its system prompt — the generated resume contained neither; only the two real, original bullets survived, and gapAnalysis still honestly listed the missing skill instead of inventing it. (3) The highest-value untested surface: a candidate's own profile content reaching an AI prompt shown to a *different* user (the employer) via `employer_match` — poisoned a synthetic candidate's own work-history bullet with the same payload, ran a real employer search against it. The score stayed a real, unmanipulated 87; the AI-authored "why" and "gaps" text cited only real, honest evidence and never repeated or acted on the injected instruction.

ONE REAL BUG: BUT THE RAW, UNMEDIATED TEXT ITSELF LEAKED THROUGH, EVEN THOUGH THE AI DIDN'T ACT ON IT. `buildProfileText()` — the function that builds both `candidate_index.profile_text` (what the AI reranker reads as evidence) and the `summary` fallback field `employer_match` returns to the client — concatenated a candidate's own work-history bullets with no length cap, the exact same shape of bug already fixed once this session in `indexCandidate()`'s `MAX_SKILL_LEN` cap, just in a sibling function. The 340-character injection bullet survived verbatim into both the stored embedding text and the API response's `summary` field. Checked severity precisely rather than assuming the worst: `EmployerHub.tsx` renders the raw `summary` field only as a fallback when the newer, structured `profile` object (which never includes raw bullets at all) is absent — for a real, current search result `profile` is always present, so this exact path is not live-reachable through today's UI. Still fixed as real defense in depth: a live attacker-controlled string sitting in a stored embedding and a documented API field is a landmine regardless of which UI branch renders it today, and it's the same "one candidate's malformed data reaches every other party unfiltered" pattern this app has already paid for once. Fixed with `MAX_BULLET_LEN = 300` (generous — no real accomplishment bullet needs anywhere near this many characters), applied where `buildProfileText` joins bullets.

Verified live, before and after: re-indexed the poisoned candidate against the deployed fix (`talent_pool_set` → `indexCandidate` → `buildProfileText`), confirmed directly against `candidate_index.profile_text` that the injected bullet was gone and only the two real bullets remained, then re-ran the identical `employer_match` call and confirmed the `summary` field in the real API response no longer contained the injected text, while the score and evidence stayed correct.

EVERYTHING ELSE CHECKED, NO REGRESSIONS. The `pg_proc` SECURITY DEFINER sweep (both `authenticated` and `anon` roles) returned exactly the two expected rows (`get_feature_flags`, `has_role`) — no new privilege-escalation gap since the last audit. `credit_grant`/`credit_spend`/`billing_ensure`/`credit_balance` confirmed still `service_role`-only, zero grant to `authenticated` or `anon`. Every table in `public` still has RLS enabled, zero exceptions. Two tables (`cookie_consent_log`, `job_cache`) have RLS enabled with zero policies *and* a real grant to `authenticated`/`anon` — read the blueprint's own warning that a grant isn't the same as access, and tested it directly rather than trusting the combination looked risky: `SET LOCAL role authenticated` against real, non-empty tables (19 and 99 rows) returned zero rows on SELECT and a real `42501` on INSERT, confirming the deny-by-default RLS-with-no-policy pattern holds even with a grant present. `stripe-webhook`'s signature verification (`constructEventAsync` against the real secret) is intact; `resend-inbound-webhook` still has no signature verification at all, a known, previously-disclosed, still-open gap — confirmed it doesn't compound into anything worse, since neither that function nor `admin-inbox-reply` ever feeds inbound-email content into an AI prompt. `save-generated-image`'s SSRF fix (exact hostname match, not substring; service-role-only) is still in place. `fetchCompanyContext`'s `isBlockedHost()` SSRF guard is still correctly gating both of its own outbound fetches (the candidate page fetch and the `robots.txt` check); the already-disclosed DNS-rebinding gap (it blocks a literal internal IP, not a public domain rebound to one) is unchanged, not newly found. `resume-hub`'s CORS is still the previously-evaluated, accepted `*` (bearer-JWT auth, not cookie-based, so the usual CSRF risk doesn't apply the same way). Grepped the whole frontend and extension for `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`/raw `innerHTML =` writes carrying AI or candidate-supplied text: the extension's own `aynFormatAiText()` HTML-escapes before any markdown-lite formatting (confirmed by reading it, not assumed), and the one raw-`innerHTML` requirement-list renderer already escapes the one character (`<`) that actually matters for tag injection, just with a less consistent style than the shared helper — noted, not treated as exploitable.

`npx tsc --noEmit` clean, `node scripts/check-wiring.mjs` clean (14 actions, unchanged). Deployed live via `supabase functions deploy resume-hub`. Every throwaway account created for this pass (one seeker used across all three injection tests, one employer, one synthetic org) fully erased afterward via `erase_account_core` plus a manual cleanup of the org/org_members/employer_searches rows erasure doesn't cascade to (the same known, standing gap this file already documents) — confirmed zero rows remaining across every table touched.

Preceded by v3.129.0, asked directly to look into the whole app for bugs as both a real user and a reviewer: found and fixed the employer-approval-bypass and suspended-account critical security holes, five more real bugs (credit-grant idempotency, a non-atomic-skills indexing bug, a silently-fabricated assessment result, an SSRF in the company-context lookup, an unescaped-subject XSS in admin email replies), two real bugs found by driving the actual seeker UI (a stale credit pill, a placeholder-title data-corruption risk), and a still-slow assessment-grading model swapped for the fast tier after nearly failing live during an end-to-end test.

CRITICAL — ANY SIGNED-IN USER, INCLUDING A PLAIN JOB SEEKER, COULD BYPASS THE EMPLOYER APPROVAL QUEUE ENTIRELY AND REACH THE REAL CANDIDATE POOL. `employer_accounts.status` — the column the admin approval queue (`admin_employer_list`/`admin_employer_decide`, v3.20.0) reads and writes — was never once read as a gate anywhere else in the file. `employer_org_create` took only `{name, website}` from any authenticated caller and inserted them as an org admin with zero check. Every downstream employer action (`employer_match`, `employer_spec_extract`, `employer_reveal_request`, assessments) only checked `assertOrgMember` — org membership, not employer approval. The company-profile-completeness gate sitting two lines above it in the same file even names the exact principle this violated, word for word: "a UI-only gate is not a gate, so every action that searches for or contacts a candidate checks here too" (v3.11.0) — the developer who wrote that sentence was solving a narrower version of the same problem and didn't carry it to employer approval itself. `EmployerPending.tsx` was real, but it was a frontend router, not a server-side check.

Reproduced live before fixing: signed up a genuine throwaway account with no employer role at all (a plain job seeker, zero `employer_accounts` rows), called `employer_org_create` directly against the deployed function with its own real session — got back a real `org.id`, `200`, no refusal. A second throwaway account that signed up as an employer but was still sitting in `pending_approval` (never touched by an admin) got the identical result.

Fixed by adding `isApprovedEmployer()` (reads `employer_accounts.status` for the caller) and calling it from inside `assertOrgMember` itself — the one function all 14 org-scoped actions already funnel through, so every one of them is now covered by a single fix — plus a standalone call at the top of `employer_org_create`, the one action that runs before any org (and therefore `assertOrgMember`) exists. Re-verified live, same two accounts, same exact request: both now get a clean `403 "Employer access is not approved for this account yet."`, and zero org was created. Then approved the pending employer for real via `employer_accounts.status = 'approved'` and re-ran the identical `employer_org_create` call — `200`, a real org, confirming the fix doesn't block the legitimate path.

CRITICAL — AN ADMIN-SUSPENDED ACCOUNT COULD KEEP USING PAID AI FEATURES ON THE WEB APP. `accountGate()` (v3.28.0, the shared suspension + capability-restriction check) is called from exactly two places: the extension lane, and one spot in the web lane's "NEW ACTIONS" block. But `parse_file`, `resume_diagnose`, `rewrite`, `guided_intake_extract`, `resume_generate`, `match`, `tailor`, `cover_letter`, and `job_fit_advice` are all separate, earlier `if (action === ...)` blocks that `return` before the dispatcher ever reaches that spot — each with its own `featureGate` (platform kill-switch) call, but none with `accountGate`. An admin suspending an account (still "all or nothing" per its own v3.28.0 design) did not actually stop that account from calling `tailor`/`match`/etc. from the web UI.

Reproduced live: suspended a real throwaway account via `account_suspensions`, called `tailor` and `match` against the deployed function with that account's own session — both returned `200`-shaped success, no `account_suspended` anywhere. Fixed by adding one `accountGate(...)` call to each of the nine affected blocks, mirroring the `featureGate` line already sitting at the top of each one (`parse_file` had no admin client of its own yet, since it never previously needed one — added a small one just for the gate). Re-verified live: the identical suspended-account calls to `tailor` and `match` now both return a clean `403 {"code":"account_suspended", ...}`. Un-suspended the same account and re-ran `match` once more to confirm the fix introduces no false positive — real `200`, real result, no mention of suspension.

Both fixes deployed live via `supabase functions deploy resume-hub`. All test accounts, orgs, and suspension rows created for this verification fully deleted after. Details in docs/map/platform.md.

FIVE MORE REAL FINDINGS FROM THE SAME REVIEW, EACH INDEPENDENTLY VERIFIED BEFORE FIXING.

`credit_grant` had zero idempotency on `_ref` — no unique constraint, no duplicate check. Stripe's own webhook delivery is documented at-least-once, so a retried `invoice.paid` event would grant the same paid credits twice. A first attempt to fix this inside `credit_grant` itself (a DB migration adding a unique index) failed loudly and safely — turned up a real, live collision: `admin_adjust_credits` passes `'admin:' || auth.uid()` as `_ref`, deliberately reused across every adjustment the same admin makes for the same user, so a blanket unique constraint would have broken real admin credit grants. Fixed instead at the one call site that actually needs it: `stripe-webhook`'s `invoice.paid` handler now checks `credit_ledger` for an existing row with that exact `invoice.id` before calling `credit_grant`, since a Stripe invoice id is genuinely globally unique. `admin_adjust_credits`'s own ref convention is untouched. Same handler also had two silently-discarded errors — the `subscriptions` upsert and the `credit_grant` call itself were never checked, so a transient DB failure on `credit_grant` still let a receipt email claiming credits were added go out. Both now checked; a failed grant logs and skips the receipt.

`employer_assessment_generate` read `e.achievements` on every candidate's work history to ground its questions in something real — `CanonicalProfile`'s experience entries have never had that field, only `bullets` (confirmed against the type itself). Every assessment has been generated with zero real detail to anchor questions to since this action shipped, despite its own prompt explicitly forbidding generic, answerable-without-knowing-this-person questions. One-line field fix.

`finaliseAssessment`'s grading call, when it threw or timed out, fell straight through into an unconditional `assessment_results` upsert — writing a real-looking `overall_score: 0`, `verification_verdict: "partly consistent"` row with no flag anywhere that grading never actually happened. A genuine candidate's real answers, scored 0/100 by a transient gateway hiccup, would have looked identical to an honest poor result to the employer making a real hiring call. Fixed by only writing a result when the model actually returned one; `employer_assessment_list` already renders a missing result as "no score yet" (the same state a normal in-flight grading call briefly sits in), so this is an existing, already-handled state, not a new one to build UI for.

`fetchCompanyContext` (the cover-letter company-lookup helper) took a client-supplied `jobUrl` and fetched it with only an ATS-hostname blocklist — no scheme check, no private/internal-IP check, so a URL like `http://169.254.169.254/` would have been fetched server-side, a real SSRF primitive. Worse, the result was cached under a key scoped to company name only (no user, no url) for 7 days — meaning one caller's own attacker-controlled URL would poison what every other real AYN user writing a cover letter to that same company got told was "the employer's own public page" for the next week. Fixed both: `isBlockedHost()` rejects loopback/link-local/RFC1918 addresses and non-http(s) schemes before any fetch (a literal internal IP, not a DNS-rebinding attack on a real domain, which would need a resolve-then-check step this doesn't do); and the 7-day shared cache now only ever gets written from the deterministic `https://www.{company-slug}.com` guess, never from a client-supplied URL — a jobUrl-sourced result still gets used for that one request, just never shared.

`admin-inbox-reply`'s reply subject is sourced verbatim from `inbound_email_replies`, itself written by `resend-inbound-webhook` from an arbitrary inbound email's own subject line — and `resend-inbound-webhook` has no signature verification of any kind (confirmed: no matching secret in the project's configured env vars, and no check in the code), so anyone who knows the webhook URL could inject a fake "inbound email" with any subject they choose. The message body was already escaped before this fix; the subject, interpolated into `heading()` with zero escaping, was not — the same bug class v3.40.0 already fixed once in `admin-broadcast`. Fixed by escaping the subject only for the HTML heading, while the literal (unescaped) subject still goes into the real email Subject header and the log, so a legitimate subject with an ampersand or apostrophe doesn't show escaped entities in a recipient's inbox. Verified live: seeded a real row with a `<script>` payload as the subject, called the deployed function for real as a genuine admin — it built and attempted to send without error (Resend's own test-domain rejection stopped the actual send, the same safe verification trick used elsewhere in this app's history). The underlying webhook-authentication gap itself is unfixed — closing it needs a signing secret configured in Resend's own dashboard first, which is outside this repo.

All five deployed live via `supabase functions deploy resume-hub` / `stripe-webhook` / `admin-inbox-reply`. Test rows and the throwaway admin account used to verify the last one fully deleted after.

THE "REAL USER" HALF OF THE PASS, DRIVEN THROUGH THE ACTUAL DEPLOYED SITE, FOUND TWO MORE. Signed in as a genuine throwaway seeker (a real session injected into a real browser tab, since this tooling still cannot type into a password field), ran a real resume through `parse_file` for real, scored and tailored against a real job description with a real, honest gap (Kubernetes/Terraform correctly flagged missing, everything present correctly credited). Two real bugs surfaced by watching the actual UI, not the API response: the header credit pill never updates after spending credits — confirmed the real ledger charge landed correctly (`-2 tailored_resume`), the pill was just stuck at the pre-spend number until a full reload, since nothing ever told it to re-check; and a manually-added job's placeholder title ("Untitled role," shown until the person edits it) could reach the tailor view's own title-mismatch suggestion card and offer a real "Use this job's title" button that would have written the literal word "Untitled role" into someone's resume. Both fixed in `JobsTab.tsx`/`ProfileTab.tsx`/`ResumeHub.tsx` — these are frontend-only changes, not deployed yet (need a git push and the site's normal rebuild, unlike the edge function fixes above which are already live).

DRIVING A REAL ASSESSMENT END TO END NEARLY CAUGHT THE FIX ABOVE FAILING FOR REAL. Answered a genuine assessment through the real UI, real timed questions, real submit. The grading call measured 143.9 seconds and came back empty — the earlier fix (only write a result when grading returns one) worked exactly as designed, correctly refusing to write a fake score, but that also meant the assessment was now silently, permanently ungraded, with no retry and nothing telling anyone it needed one. Root cause: assessment grading was still calling `QUALITY_MODEL` (gemini-2.5-pro), the same model already measured causing real failures on three other call sites (v3.96.0/v3.97.0) — that same pass explicitly flagged assessment grading as untested and left it on the slow tier. It's now confirmed both slow and, at 143.9s, right at the edge of this app's own documented 150s ceiling. A blind retry was tried first and deliberately reverted — a second ~144s attempt risked pushing the whole request past a hard timeout, trading an honest "not graded" for a real chance of outright failure. Fixed the proven way instead: switched the grading call to `DEFAULT_MODEL` (flash tier), the same fix already proven for `rewrite`/`tailor`/`smart_tailor`. Verified live with a second real assessment, same candidate: submit-to-graded dropped from 143.9s to 5s, and grading quality held — a real 85/100, "consistent," and an employer summary citing the candidate's actual answers.

A REAL, LIVE DATA-QUALITY BUG FOUND BY ACCIDENT WHILE TESTING THE EMPLOYER SIDE — AND IT WAS THE FOUNDER'S OWN ACCOUNT. Signed in as a real, approved throwaway employer and ran the actual search intake wizard. The "nice to have skills" suggestion step showed chips that were not skill names at all — full paragraphs like "Applied AI: Built and shipped AYN (aynn.io), a production AI platform. LLM integration and orchestration, prompt engineering, RAG, evaluation and quality, latency and cost optimization, guardrails, responsible AI." (213 characters). Traced it: one real, live, opted-in candidate had `candidate_skills` rows shaped exactly like the "Technical Stack: React, TypeScript..." non-atomic-skill bug this file's own v3.65.0 entry already documents fixing once, in the `rewrite`/`resume_generate` writing prompts specifically — but `indexCandidate()` (the function that rebuilds `candidate_skills`, the one table `employer_skill_catalog` reads to suggest skills to every employer during search) has no defensive check of its own; it indexes whatever shape `canonical.skills`/a resume's own skills array already has, including data from before that prompt rule existed or from any path that never went through those two prompts. The account turned out to be `crossmint7@gmail.com` — this file's own v3.77.0 entry names it as the founder's first, real account — meaning this wasn't synthetic test data, it was live, and it was surfacing garbled in front of real employers using the product right now.

Fixed with a defensive length cap in `indexCandidate()` itself (`MAX_SKILL_LEN = 60`, applied to canonical skills, resume skills, and inferred top-skills alike): nothing legitimate is a 200-character sentence, so refusing to index anything longer costs nothing real while closing this off regardless of which upstream path produced it, past or future. Deployed live via `supabase functions deploy resume-hub`. The founder's own candidate row was marked stale (`indexed_at`/`embedding_model` nulled, the same mechanism `admin_mark_candidates_stale` already uses) so it re-indexes clean the next time it's touched — either he clicks "Re-index my profile" himself, or the next employer search that reaches his record picks it up automatically; deliberately did not call the reindex action on his behalf, since that requires his own session, not something to simulate.

Preceded by v3.128.0, asked directly to also check the Developer Terms of Service, the full Program Policies index, and the Branding guidelines against this extension's code, then specifically re-checked the "Use of Permissions" policy section.

TERMS/BRANDING/SPAM-AND-ABUSE: ALL CLEAN, NO CODE CHANGE NEEDED. Local storage (`chrome.storage.local`: device token, last-detected job, a pending-resume marker) is fully wiped on sign-out (`SIGN_OUT` handler calls `chrome.storage.local.clear()`, comment: "wipe ALL local data for privacy"), satisfying the Terms' "only for as long as it is needed." No `chrome.notifications` usage anywhere, so the spam/notification-abuse policy doesn't apply. No Google trademark or logo use — the extension's own name and icon (the AYN eye mark) are unrelated to Google branding, and the manifest description doesn't even mention "Chrome," so the trademark-attribution-format rule doesn't apply either. No device-setting changes anywhere in the code. Grepped the whole `extension/` tree for the old `aynn.io` domain (retired in the v3.85.0 migration) and found zero leftover references — every in-extension link (billing, privacy, error messages) correctly points at `ayn.careers`.

ONE MORE REDUNDANT PERMISSION FOUND AND REMOVED, LOWER STAKES THAN `webNavigation` BUT THE SAME SHAPE. Re-reading "Use of Permissions" ("request the narrowest permissions necessary... don't request a permission that might benefit features not yet implemented") prompted a second look at `activeTab`. It grants *temporary* per-invocation access to a tab; this manifest already holds `host_permissions: ["https://*/*"]`, *permanent* access to every HTTPS page — so `activeTab` adds nothing on top of it. Confirmed by reading every `chrome.tabs.*`/`chrome.scripting.*` call in `background.js`: none of them depend on activeTab specifically, all are already covered by the standing host permission. Unlike `webNavigation`, Google's own review did NOT name this as a problem — it's optional cleanup, not a fix to a real defect, flagged rather than silently changed; removed once asked. Version bumped to 3.4.5, rebuilt via `node extension/build.mjs`, `check-wiring` clean.

Preceded by v3.127.0, asked directly to read Google's actual Chrome Web Store developer policies (Developer Program Policies plus the troubleshooting/excessive-permissions page) and check this extension's real code against them, not just the two violations the v3.126.0 rejection already named.

PERMISSIONS, REMOTE CODE, CODE READABILITY, WEB BROWSING ACTIVITY: ALL CONFIRMED CLEAN. `manifest.json`'s three permissions (`activeTab`, `storage`, `sidePanel`) are each confirmed actually used by grep; `host_permissions`/`content_scripts` on `https://*/*` is broad but load-bearing, not "future-proofing" — job postings live on an unbounded set of ATS/company domains, not a fixed list. The v3.126.0 remote-code fix (above) is confirmed still clean in the packaged zip. Every hand-written file (`background.js`, `content.js`, `sidepanel.js`) is plain, readable, non-minified; the vendor bundles are minified (allowed) but not obfuscated — the only base64-shaped blobs in `vendor/jspdf.umd.min.js` are jsPDF's own standard-font width/kerning tables, confirmed by reading the surrounding code, not disguised logic. Read `detectAndReport()`/`resolveJdForTab()` in `background.js` to check the "web browsing activity" policy specifically: automatic page reading is gated to a fixed regex allowlist of known job-board hostnames (`JOB_PAGE_RE`), the coarse per-tab classification that DOES run on every page (`SET_TAB_KIND`) never leaves an in-memory `Map` in the background worker, and a full page-content read on an arbitrary site only ever happens from `RESOLVE_JD`, "the public entry point for the sidepanel" — i.e., a user-invoked action, not passive collection.

ONE REAL, LIVE POLICY GAP FOUND AND FIXED: THE EXTENSION'S OWN CONSENT SCREEN DESCRIBED A FEATURE IT DOESN'T HAVE. Google's "Prominent Disclosure" policy requires disclosure of what user data will be collected and how, inside the product's own UI, before collection starts, and requires that disclosure to be accurate. `src/pages/ExtensionApprove.tsx` — the actual consent screen shown when a browser is linked to an AYN account, the one moment this policy is really about — read "This browser will be allowed to fill job applications, score jobs, and tailor your resume on your behalf." "Fill job applications" describes the old autofill feature, deleted app-wide in v3.0.0; the extension has been read-only for dozens of versions since. A user approving a browser link has been told, at the one moment Google's policy cares about most, that the extension does something it explicitly does not do. Fixed the copy to state plainly what the extension actually does (reads job postings, scores and tailors) and that it is read-only and never fills or submits anything. Also added a small always-visible shield icon in the side panel's own header (`sidepanel.html`/`.js`, reusing the extension's own already-shipped `ti-shield-check` glyph rather than guessing a codepoint outside the subsetted icon font, confirmed by grep against `fonts/tabler-icons.css` before using it) linking to `ayn.careers/privacy`, so the disclosure lives inside the extension's own UI too, not only on the website — even though `src/content/legal/privacy.md` already had an accurate, extension-specific paragraph describing exactly what the extension reads and sends (confirmed by reading it, not assumed).

Verified live: the shield icon renders correctly (not a missing-glyph box) via a local static server and a real browser screenshot; `npx tsc --noEmit` clean for the `.tsx` change, `node --check` clean for the `.js` change, `check-wiring` clean. Version bumped to 3.4.4, rebuilt via `node extension/build.mjs`. Not fixed here, flagged for a human to check separately since it lives outside this repo: the actual Chrome Web Store listing text (title, description, screenshots) is a Developer Dashboard field, not code, and wasn't reviewable from here.

Preceded by v3.126.0, the Chrome Web Store rejected the pending extension submission (v3.4.0) on two grounds: an already-fixed `webNavigation` permission (v3.86.0, reviewing a stale build), and "Including remotely hosted code in a Manifest V3 item" — real, traced to a literal cause, not guessed at.

`extension/vendor/jspdf.umd.min.js`, a hand-vendored jsPDF 2.5.2 build, has an `output()` mode (`"pdfobjectnewwindow"`, never called by this app — `resumeFormat.js` only ever calls `output('blob')`) that builds a script tag pointed at `cdnjs.cloudflare.com` and writes it into a new window: a genuine, literal remote-code load shipped in the package regardless of whether the app ever exercises it. `extension/vendor/docx.min.js`, via its bundled `jszip` dependency's legacy `setimmediate`/`asap` polyfills, separately carried two IE-era fallbacks that build a script element and one that turns a string into a function — both provably unreachable in real Chrome (Chrome always has `MutationObserver`/`postMessage`, which win first in the same fallback chains) but still present as literal text, which Chrome's automated scanner flags by presence, not reachability.

Fixed by patching both vendor files directly rather than rebuilding from npm — `docx`'s own current npm dist (`node_modules/docx/dist/index.mjs`) carries the identical dead code, so a version bump alone would not have helped. The dead output mode now throws instead of building a remote script tag; the two legacy scheduling fallbacks were replaced with the plain `setTimeout` branch each already had, a behavior-preserving substitution since Chrome never reached those branches anyway. Verified grep-clean of all three flagged patterns in the packaged zip, `node --check` clean on both files, and live in a real browser: PDF generation via `output('blob')` and DOCX generation via `Packer.toBlob()` — the only two codepaths this app actually calls — both still produce valid one-page documents after the patch. Version bumped to 3.4.3, rebuilt via `node extension/build.mjs`. Details in docs/map/extension.md.

Preceded by v3.125.0, asked directly to test everything in the system end to end. One real, live honesty bug found and fixed: `tailor`, `cover_letter`, `match`, `smart_tailor` and every other action sharing `_shared/identity.ts` could still fabricate a placeholder name for a brand-new account, the exact "Ayn User" failure class this file has documented fixing once already — just never fixed at its actual shared root.

`loadIdentity()` HAD NO AUTH-METADATA FALLBACK FOR NAME, ONLY FOR EMAIL. Reproduced live, not guessed at: signed up a genuine throwaway seeker, saved a canonical profile (skills/experience/education, no top-level name field — the schema doesn't have one), then called `tailor` before ever generating or uploading a primary resume. It returned `basics.name: "A. Developer"` — invented, not a placeholder string like "Your Name," a plausible-sounding fake identity, indistinguishable from a real one to anyone who didn't already know better. Traced the cause: `loadIdentity()` (`_shared/identity.ts`), the one shared function `tailor`, `cover_letter`, `match`, `job_fit_advice`, `smart_tailor` and `ext_cover_letter_text` all load applicant identity through, picks `full_name` from profile, then canonical, then resume `basics.name` — and stops there. `email` has always had a fourth fallback, `auth.users.email` (the v2.13.0 comment even names the exact bug it closed: "the missing fallback that made 'Isha Sharma' possible"). `full_name`/`first_name`/`last_name` never got the equivalent fallback to `auth.users.user_metadata.full_name` — the real name typed at signup, sitting right there the whole time. `resume_generate` had already been patched for this exact scenario once (v3.120.0, its own bespoke inline fallback, not this shared function), which is exactly why it correctly returned "E2E Seeker" in the same test run while `tailor`, called moments later on the same account, invented a name.

Fixed at the actual shared root instead of patching another individual call site: `loadIdentity()` now reads `user_metadata.full_name` from the same `auth.admin.getUserById` call it already makes for email, splits it the same way resume `basics.name` already gets split for first/last, and adds it as the final fallback for `first_name`, `last_name`, and `full_name` — one fix, every consumer of this file benefits, no per-action patch needed. Added a matching explicit rule to `tailor`'s own system prompt too (rule 6b, mirroring how rule 6 already protects `basics.title`) as defense in depth, plus the identical one-line protection added to `rewrite`'s rule 3, since `rewrite` takes a client-supplied resume directly and had no explicit "never alter or invent basics.name" instruction either, just an implicit one.

Verified live, twice: re-ran the identical `tailor` call against the identical account state (still no primary resume) after redeploying — `basics.name` now correctly returns "E2E Seeker," and `ext_bootstrap`'s own identity summary now reports `first_name`/`last_name` sourced from `"auth"` instead of `"missing"`. Re-confirmed on `smart_tailor` and `ext_cover_letter_text` (the extension's own equivalents, both consumers of the same shared function) — both now correctly sign the real name instead of inventing one.

THE REST OF THE PASS HELD, NO OTHER BUGS FOUND. Full pipeline run against real throwaway accounts, cleaned up after: `parse_file` (real DOCX-shaped plain text, every figure preserved exactly, education/certifications correctly split), `profile_canonical_save`, `resume_generate` (15 credits), `match` (correctly named the one genuine gap, Terraform, out of a JD with several stated requirements), `tailor` and `cover_letter` (both now correctly signed), `talent_pool_set` (real embedding indexed, skills tagged `extracted`), the full employer side (`employer_spec_extract` grounded to only what was stated, `employer_match` found the seeded candidate at score 87 with an honest stated seniority gap, not glossed over), the full proposal loop (`employer_reveal_request` → seeker sees the real employer profile immediately → `reveal_decide` accept → employer receives the real email, name/phone correctly stay null since never provided), the full assessment loop (questions grounded in the candidate's real claimed employers, and the AI grader correctly flagged my own synthetic answers — six questions each timed at an identical, artificial 8 seconds — as suspicious, `verification_verdict: "inconsistent"`, exactly the honesty-under-pressure behavior this feature exists to catch), the credit gate (a drained-to-zero account got a clean `402 insufficient_credits` with no partial charge), and the full extension device-token lane (`link_start`/`link_approve`/`link_poll`, `ext_bootstrap`, `ext_job_score`, `token_list`/`token_revoke`). The landing page's own recent changes (hero rework, the toggle-scroll fix) were re-confirmed live on production too.

`npx tsc --noEmit` and `check-wiring` both clean. Deployed live via `supabase functions deploy resume-hub`. Every throwaway account (two real signups plus one admin-flagged account), the synthetic org, and one leftover assessment row the erasure flow doesn't cascade to (an org a user merely created, same known gap this file already documents) were all fully erased, purged, and manually swept afterward — confirmed zero rows remaining across every table touched.

Preceded by v3.124.0, asked directly to make AYN "smart" — picked all three of: real semantic understanding (not just word-matching), self-verification (catch a mistake in code before a human has to), and real judgment. All three built and verified live; the third scoped deliberately, not a rebuild of the free-form chat this app already tried and removed once.

SELF-VERIFICATION: EVERY RESUME-WRITING CALL NOW CHECKS ITS OWN OUTPUT IN CODE BEFORE A USER SEES IT. Every real bug this app's own writing has ever had — the fabricated "Ayn User" placeholder, scores that bounced on identical input, today's earlier certifications duplication — was caught by a human testing afterward, never by the model noticing its own mistake. New shared `verifyWriteQuality()`/`verifyProseQuality()`/`violationsToRetryNote()` in `_shared/tailoring.ts` check, deterministically, in code: every figure preserved (extending the check `tailor` already had to `rewrite` and `resume_generate`, which never had it before), zero banned cliché phrases, zero first-person pronouns (resumes only — a cover letter is legitimately first person, `verifyProseQuality`'s `checkPronouns` param defaults off for that surface), zero em/en dashes. Wired into `rewrite`, `resume_generate`, `tailor`, the extension's `smart_tailor`, and both cover-letter paths (web `cover_letter`, shared `handleCoverLetter`) — one retry naming every violation found in a single round trip, keeping the retry only if it's genuinely better, same pattern `tailor`'s own figure check already used. This runs on every real write from now on; most of the time it finds nothing (a good sign the prompts already comply), but the check itself is now permanent, not just a prompt request.

SEMANTIC GAP-MATCHING: REAL MEANING, NOT JUST A CURATED SYNONYM LIST. The v3.122.0 fix added ~20 hand-picked synonym pairs (Adobe Creative Suite/Cloud, Postgres/PostgreSQL, etc.) to the deterministic matcher; asked directly for the real fix, not more curation. New `embedBatch()` (resume-hub/index.ts) and `applySemanticRecheck()`/`cosineSimilarity()` (`_shared/tailoring.ts`): after the deterministic matcher calls a requirement "missing," a second pass embeds it and compares against the candidate's own skills and bullets — code-computed cosine similarity, not a model's opinion, same "code decides, model phrases" rule as everywhere else in this app. Wired into the three seeker-facing surfaces that show a gap analysis: `match` (scoring), `tailor`, `cover_letter`. Only promotes a false "missing" to "matched," never the reverse.

TWO REAL BUGS FOUND BUILDING THIS, BOTH CAUGHT BY TESTING LIVE, NOT BY READING THE CODE. One: the first version of `embedBatch` tried the embeddings endpoint's array-input form to do a whole batch in one request; live testing showed it silently falling back to hash embeddings every time — the gateway's route doesn't accept `input` as an array the way raw OpenAI's does, and both the batch call's fallback AND the semantic recheck's own "skip if not real embeddings" guard fired silently, so nothing crashed, it just quietly never worked. Fixed by fanning out to `embedText()` (already proven working — real `candidate_index` rows carry `embedding_model: 'openai/text-embedding-3-small'`) with `Promise.all`, one call per text, capped at ~32 concurrent requests.

Two, once real embeddings were actually flowing: the initial similarity threshold (0.72) was a guess, and it was wrong. Live-tested against a genuine, fair match ("Interface mockup software" as a JD requirement against a resume's own "Prototyping" skill — Figma-style tools ARE interface mockup software) scored 0.526, while two genuinely unrelated pairs scored 0.29 and 0.31. text-embedding-3-small's cosine similarity on short phrases runs lower than intuition suggests. Recalibrated to 0.5 — real margin above the confirmed negatives, just below the one confirmed positive found so far. Re-verified live post-fix: the same "Interface mockup software" JD requirement now correctly drops out of `gapAnalysis.missing` while the two genuinely absent requirements ("Blockchain smart contract development," "Team leadership") correctly stay listed.

A THIRD, REAL BUG FOUND BUILDING THE NEXT PIECE: SHORT TOOL NAMES BROKE THE SEMANTIC MATCH IN THE OPPOSITE DIRECTION. Building `job_fit_advice` (below) surfaced a live false positive — "Kubernetes" got promoted to "matched" against a resume that only had "Docker," with the model then telling the person "you have Kubernetes experience" when they don't. Measured the actual cause rather than guessing: Docker/Kubernetes scored 0.559 cosine similarity, HIGHER than the genuine "Interface mockup software"/"Prototyping" match (0.526) that justified the 0.5 threshold in the first place. Short, commonly co-occurring tech names (Docker and Kubernetes are used together constantly) get pulled close in embedding space by topical association, not semantic identity — no single threshold can separate "the same thing, worded differently" from "a related but different tool" when the wrong pair scores higher than the right one. Fixed by excluding bare 1-2 word requirements from semantic promotion entirely (`applySemanticRecheck` now requires 3+ words): short tool names are already handled correctly by the literal + curated-synonym matcher, which doesn't confuse adjacent technologies; only longer, descriptive phrases (which carry enough real content to disambiguate) go through the semantic check. Re-verified live post-fix: the identical Docker/Kubernetes case now correctly leaves Kubernetes in `missing`, while the "Interface mockup software" case still correctly promotes.

REAL JUDGMENT, DELIBERATELY NOT A REBUILD OF THE ORIGINAL SEEKER CHAT. This app already tried an open-ended, judgment-giving AI surface once — the very first seeker product was a free-form career chat, deleted in v3.8.0 for producing confident-sounding, ungrounded flattery and offering capabilities the product didn't have. That failure is why the architecture since then has been "code decides facts, the model only phrases them." Flagged this directly before building anything narrower in its place. What shipped: a new free action, `job_fit_advice`, on a new "Should I apply?" button in the Jobs tab (shown once a job is scored). The verdict — strong_fit / worth_trying / significant_gaps / no_stated_requirements — is computed in code from the same gap analysis `tailor`/`match` already produce (coverage = matched / (matched + missing) required items), never chosen by the model; the AI's only job is to explain a verdict it did not pick, citing only the matched/missing items it was given, explicitly forbidden from promising an outcome, inventing enthusiasm, or suggesting anything outside what this product actually does (no interview coaching, no salary advice, no career planning — the exact shape of the v3.8.0 failure). It is allowed, deliberately, to tell someone a role may not be worth their time — verified live saying exactly that on a genuinely poor-fit case, and verified separately being plainly positive on a genuine strong fit and honestly declining to guess on a posting with no checkable requirements.

`npx tsc --noEmit` and `node scripts/check-wiring.mjs` both clean after every edit (14 hub actions, up from 13). Deployed live via `supabase functions deploy resume-hub` multiple times (including three separate live-testing rounds that found and fixed the embedBatch bug, the threshold miscalibration, and the Docker/Kubernetes false positive — all three caught by testing the actual deployed behavior, not by reading the code back). All test accounts and their seeded credits fully erased after via `erase_account_core`; temporary debug fields and `console.log` lines used to diagnose each of the three bugs were added, used, and fully removed before the final deploy — never shipped.

Preceded by v3.123.0, asked directly to build 10 specific gaps the v3.122.0 comparison had flagged as not-yet-built, then test it.

8 OF 10 BUILT LIVE; 2 CONFIRMED AS REAL, SEPARATE FEATURES RATHER THAN RUSHED. The founder picked from the v3.122.0 gap list and asked for all of it. Two (aggregating 3 to 5 job postings before tailoring; a broken-system debugging question type for assessments) each need real new UI or a new content type, not a prompt edit — building a shallow version of either just to check a box would have shipped something half-working, so both were left explicitly undone rather than faked.

FORMATTING, ALL FOUR VERIFIED AGAINST THE ACTUAL RENDERED PDF, NOT JUST READ: `resumeDocs.ts` line spacing tightened from 1.28 to 1.15; header size bumped from barely-bigger-than-body to a real 13pt against a 10.5pt body (within the requested 12 to 14pt range); section-gap-after-header retuned from 4pt to 7pt (within the requested 6 to 8pt range); and a genuine one-page exception for executive titles (Chief/CEO/CTO/COO/CFO/President/VP/Executive Director/Managing Director, matched off `basics.title`) that skips the shrink-to-fit ladder entirely and lets the existing page-break safety net produce a real second page at a comfortable 10 to 10.5pt instead of forcing everything down to 8pt. Honest gap: there's no way to detect "academic researcher with a publications list" from this schema at all — no publications field exists anywhere — so only the executive half of that exception is real; the academic half was not faked. Verified live: a seeded 8-role, 20-year executive resume held at 10.5pt through 6 roles (fit on one real page at the tighter spacing alone), then correctly spilled to a genuine second page, still at 10.5pt, once 2 more roles pushed it past what even the loosened spacing could hold — confirmed by reading the actual `/Count` and `/Tf` values out of the generated PDF bytes, not by eyeballing a screenshot.

WRITING RULES, VERIFIED BY WHAT THE MODEL ACTUALLY WROTE: a hard 1-to-2-sentence cap on every resume's summary, added to `rewrite`, `tailor`, `resume_generate`, and the extension's `TAILOR_RULES`. A jargon-translation rule (rephrase an internal-only company term or project codename into plain, industry-standard language — never invent what the internal thing was, only rephrase). Verified live on a resume seeded with a bullet reading "Migrated the checkout system onto our internal Project Falcon framework" — `rewrite` correctly returned "Migrated the checkout system to an internal framework," dropping the invented-sounding codename without inventing a replacement fact, and the same run's summary held to exactly two sentences.

SYNONYM-AWARE GAP MATCHING, THE REAL ARCHITECTURE FIX FROM THE RESEARCH PASS: `_shared/tailoring.ts`'s deterministic "what's missing" checker was literal-word matching only — "Adobe Creative Suite" on a resume against a JD asking for "Adobe Creative Cloud" registered as a genuine gap. Fixed additively: a curated table of about 20 well-established, unambiguous synonym groups (Adobe Creative Suite/Cloud, Postgres/PostgreSQL, AWS/Amazon Web Services, CI/CD spelled out, and similar) expands the matched-resume-text haystack with every synonym of whatever's already present, before the existing per-term coverage math runs — so it can only turn a false "missing" into a correct "matched," never narrow what already worked. Verified live: seeded a resume with "Adobe Creative Suite" in skills, ran `tailor` against a JD requiring "Adobe Creative Cloud" — the returned gap analysis correctly did not list it as missing, while a genuinely absent requirement (Kubernetes) still correctly did.

IMPACT-TO-TENURE RATIO IN EMPLOYER SEARCH: `employer_match` now counts quantified results (numbers, percentages, dollar/scale figures) in each candidate's own profile text and divides by their years of experience — a deterministic, code-computed signal, not a model guess, matching this file's own standing design rule that the model never discovers a fact, code does. Folded into the rerank prompt as one more real signal alongside skills and seniority fit, explicitly told never to override a clear mismatch, and surfaced on the returned card. Honest gap: this one compiled clean, deployed, and was checked by code review, but a full live `employer_match` round trip in the test environment kept returning zero results for reasons upstream of this change (the synthetic embedding vector used to seed a fake candidate for testing didn't produce a usable similarity match through `match_candidates_by_embedding`) — confirmed the failure sits before this diff's own code runs (candidate_index rows were unaffected by the calls that failed), but did not get a clean end-to-end confirmation the way the other seven items got. Flagged rather than claimed.

`npx tsc --noEmit` clean after every edit. Deployed live via `supabase functions deploy resume-hub`. All seeded test accounts (an executive-resume seeker, an employer with a synthetic org) fully erased after via `erase_account_core`, plus the orphaned test org and its rows manually removed since account erasure doesn't cascade to an org a user merely created.

Preceded by v3.122.0, asked directly to research how the best resumes are actually built in 2026 and compare it to AYN, then given a large, detailed playbook spec and told to build it in.

A SCOPED SUBSET OF A FOUNDER-SUPPLIED "MASTER PLAYBOOK" WAS FOLDED INTO THE SIX PLACES THAT WRITE RESUME OR COVER LETTER PROSE, NOT ADOPTED WHOLESALE. The research pass first (web search, current 2026 sources) found AYN already ahead of the field in ways nobody had asked for credit on: the no-em-dash house rule turns out to double as AI-detection evasion (recruiters are now specifically trained to spot em-dash overuse as an AI tell), the refusal to invent an explanation for an employment gap matches exactly what recruiters are now trained to catch AI resumes doing, and the Assessments feature already anticipated the real 2026 shift toward skills-validation over keyword-matching. That research also surfaced one real, evidence-backed disagreement with the product's own one-page policy (current data: 90% of recruiters now prefer two pages for 3+ years of experience) — flagged directly rather than silently changed. The founder's own follow-up playbook explicitly reaffirmed the one-page rule with a named exception (C-level/academic), which is the more specific, deliberate instruction and is what shipped; the two-page recommendation was not implemented.

From the playbook, implemented across `rewrite`, `tailor` (web), `TAILOR_RULES` (extension's `smart_tailor`), and `resume_generate`: bullets now shaped as Accomplished-[X]-measured-by-[Y]-by-doing-[Z] wherever the underlying fact supports it (reinforcing, not replacing, the existing verb+number rule); an explicit no-pronoun rule ("I"/"me"/"my"/"we") and a present-tense-for-current/past-tense-for-past-roles rule; a "Selected Prior Experience" style trim — beyond about 5 roles or 10 to 15 years back, only the most recent, most relevant roles keep full bullets, the rest compress to one line each; and the banned-phrase list grew to include the specific words 2026 recruiter training now flags as AI tells ("realm", "intricate", "showcasing", "pivotal", "delve", "synergy", "hard-working", "detail-oriented"), not just the original "results-driven"/"leveraging" set. `ATS_RUBRIC` (shared by `resume_diagnose` and `rewrite`'s own score) gained two new concrete, checkable deductions for the same two things: a pronoun anywhere, and a tense mismatch on the current or a past role. `resumeDocs.ts` gained an explicit `SUMMARY` header (previously the summary paragraph had no header at all), matching the "standard heading dictionary" the playbook calls for. Both cover-letter prompts (`cover_letter` web, `handleCoverLetter` shared by the extension) got explicit per-paragraph word targets (hook ~50, proof ~100, alignment ~75, close ~40, 250 to 300 words total) and the same expanded banned-phrase list. `employer_assessment_generate` gained a sixth required question shape: a real, named constraint from the candidate's own claimed work (their actual stack, scale, team size) and how they'd adapt one of their own decisions if it changed — grounded in what they actually claimed, not a fictional company detail, since a generic model can fake a plausible answer to a fictional constraint but not the specific adaptation someone who lived a real one would give.

Deliberately NOT built, and why: aggregating 3 to 5 job descriptions to reverse-engineer keyword frequency (AYN's architecture tailors against one job at a time; this is a real, separate feature, not a prompt tweak) — flagged as a future idea, not silently dropped. A live video/screen-share defense round for assessments (a real product idea, but a different feature surface than an AI-graded async assessment). File-metadata/naming tricks (already covered — `fileBase()` already produces `FirstName_LastName_Resume` style names). The proposed 5-pillar 0-100 rescoring model was NOT adopted in place of `ATS_RUBRIC`'s existing flat point-deduction system — that system was deliberately hardened once already (v3.66.0) specifically to stop the score from bouncing on identical input, and a weighted-pillar reframe reintroduces the same "vibes, not arithmetic" risk that bug already proved out. The new checks were added as more deterministic deductions inside the existing system instead.

ONE REAL BUG SURFACED DURING VERIFICATION, NOT BY READING THE PROMPT: the mutual-exclusivity instruction from the v3.121.0 certifications fix ("goes in certifications, never education") was not strong enough on its own. Live-tested against the founder's real resume a second time: the model correctly added the Wharton/Coursera specialization to `certifications` but also left a copy sitting in `education` — satisfying "certifications is the right place for this" without treating it as "and therefore not also in education." A first strengthening pass ("MOVE it, remove it from education entirely") still didn't hold on the next live run. What actually worked: a concrete worked example inside the prompt showing the exact wrong output next to the exact right one, plus an explicit self-check instruction ("no school/program name may appear in both arrays") — abstract instructions describing the rule were not enough, the model needed to see the specific failure shape spelled out. Applied to all six education/certifications call sites for consistency, not just the one that failed. Re-verified live, twice: the founder's real resume optimized through the actual UI now shows the degree in `education` and the specialization in `certifications` with zero overlap, confirmed directly against `resumes.content`, and the downloaded PDF renders SUMMARY, SKILLS, EXPERIENCE, CERTIFICATIONS, and EDUCATION as five distinct headed sections.

`npx tsc --noEmit` clean after each of the three deploys. Deployed live via `supabase functions deploy resume-hub` three times (initial pass, mutual-exclusivity fix, strengthened worked-example fix), tested live between each rather than batched. Test accounts and all seeded credits fully erased after via `erase_account_core`; temporary public/ files and the extra local dev server used for verification both removed/stopped.

Preceded by v3.121.0, asked to run the founder's own real resume through AYN live, the optimizer and the from-scratch builder side by side using the same underlying facts, to show what actually changes and how capable the product is.

REAL PROOF, REAL RESUME, ONE HONEST ARTIFACT OF THE TEST ITSELF. The founder's own real `.docx` (not a synthetic fixture) went through `parse_file`, `rewrite`, and separately `resume_generate` live, and the three resulting PDFs were sent to him directly. A DOCX-parsing failure that surfaced mid-test looked at first like a Deno/mammoth compatibility bug ("Corrupted zip: missing 2295 bytes") but traced back to this session's own tooling, not the product: a base64 string hand-pasted across multiple tool calls got silently truncated. Confirmed by round-tripping the same file's real base64 through Python (came back the correct 10929 bytes every time) and switching to a byte-exact transfer method (serving the file from Vite's `public/` directory and fetching it in-browser) instead of manual base64 transcription, which then parsed the same file cleanly. Two smaller, real improvements were kept from the investigation regardless, since both are genuine fixes even though neither was the actual root cause: mammoth's DOCX extraction now wraps bytes in a real `node:buffer` Buffer (matches what the library's own Deno npm-compat layer actually checks for, rather than a bare `Uint8Array`), and a DOCX that still can't be read no longer falls through to a doomed Stage 3 vision-API call (Google's file API flatly rejects the wordprocessingml MIME type) — it now returns the same clean, honest error a genuinely unreadable file already gets everywhere else in this function, instead of a raw upstream error.

A REAL, LIVE, FIVE-PLACE BUG FOUND BY LOOKING AT THE ACTUAL OUTPUT: EVERY NON-DEGREE CREDENTIAL WAS BEING FILED AS EDUCATION. Reported directly, from the actual downloaded PDFs: the founder's real Coursera/Wharton "AI for Business" specialization was rendering merged into the EDUCATION section on all three of the as-uploaded, optimized, and from-scratch documents, with no separate CERTIFICATIONS heading ever appearing, even though `resumeDocs.ts` has rendered a distinct CERTIFICATIONS section since v3.95.0 and `ProfileTab` has had a real Certificates form since v3.70.0. Root cause: nowhere in the five places that classify resume content into education vs. certifications (`parse_file`'s Stage 2 text-path extraction, `parse_file`'s Stage 3 vision extraction, `canonicalizeProfile`'s `CANONICAL_SCHEMA` prompt, `rewrite`, `resume_generate`) was the model ever told the difference — every prompt said "extract education" and "extract certifications" with no rule for which one a non-degree credential belongs in, so a specialization sitting under a resume's own "Education" heading (a common real-world formatting choice) got extracted as an education entry every time, and `guided_intake_extract`'s interview never even asked about certifications separately. Fixed with one consistent rule added to all five prompts (education means degree-granting programs only: Bachelor's, Master's, Associate's, PhD, diploma; everything else, a professional certificate, online specialization, bootcamp, license, or short course, goes in certifications, even when the source document lists both under one heading), plus `rewrite` gained an explicit instruction to correct a misclassification it finds already sitting in an uploaded resume, not just avoid making a new one. `GuidedIntake.tsx` gained a fourth, optional interview question ("Any certifications, licenses, or completed courses?"), since the intake previously had no way to even surface this content, with a "Skip" affordance since this is the one genuinely optional step.

Verified live against the exact resume that surfaced the bug, not a synthetic case: re-uploaded the founder's real `.docx` through a fresh throwaway account post-deploy. `resumes.content` now correctly separates the one real degree (`education: [{degree: "Bachelor of Commerce", school: "Saint Mary's University, Halifax"}]`) from the Coursera/Wharton specialization (`certifications: ["AI for Business, Online Specialization (Coursera) | Wharton School..."]`), confirmed directly against the database, not just the UI. The downloaded PDF was captured with the same Blob-interception technique used for the live demo and confirmed to contain both a CERTIFICATIONS and an EDUCATION heading as two distinct sections, not one merged block. `npx tsc --noEmit` clean, deployed live via `supabase functions deploy resume-hub`. Test account fully erased after via `erase_account_core`.

Preceded by v3.120.0, asked directly to build a resume from scratch for a seeker who has none, using the same document pipeline and format the optimizer already produces.

A GUIDED INTERVIEW REPLACES THE UPLOAD STEP, AND A NEW PAID ACTION BUILDS THE DOCUMENT FROM THE PROFILE ALONE. Deliberately not a blank Profile form: describing your own work history for the first time is hard to do into "Title / Company / Start / End" fields cold, so `GuidedIntake.tsx` asks in plain language instead, one question at a time (most recent role, looped with "anything before that", then education, then skills), mirroring the employer-side intake wizard's already-proven "plain question, not a bare form" pattern. One AI call at the end, the new free `guided_intake_extract` action, structures the raw answers into the exact same career shape (`skills`/`experiences`/`education`/`certifications`/`derived`) `ProfileTab.tsx` already edits — so review is just the normal Skills/Work history/Education sections, pre-filled instead of blank, not new UI. `handleIntakeComplete` merges the result into `career` state and autosaves, same as an upload's `mapResumeToCareer`.

Once there is profile content but no resume, a new "Generate my resume — 15 credits" button calls `resume_generate`, priced and gated identically to `rewrite` (same `COST_OPTIMIZE`, same `assertCredits`/`creditSpend`) since it is the same class of action: AI writing real prose, not free profile editing. The backend resolves the caller's own canonical profile server side via the existing `loadCanonical()` (the same resolver `match`/`tailor` already use, so the client can't send stale data), and writes a complete resume in `RESUME_SCHEMA` under the same honesty rules `rewrite` already enforces (never invent an employer, title, date, number, or skill). The result lands in `resumes` through the identical `is_primary` swap `optimizeResume()` uses, so it automatically gets the same one-page ATS-formatted PDF/DOCX build (`buildResumePdfBlob`/`buildResumeDocxBlob`), the same scoring, and the same tailoring/extension pipeline as any uploaded resume — no new rendering path needed.

ONE REAL HONESTY BUG FOUND AND FIXED DURING LIVE VERIFICATION. A fresh throwaway account with a filled-in profile but no `user_profile_data` row yet (that table only gets written the first time someone edits "About you") generated a resume with `basics.name: "Ayn User"` — the model, faced with a required schema field and no real name in scope, invented a plausible-sounding placeholder instead of leaving it honest. Fixed by falling back to the real name already sitting in `auth.users.raw_user_meta_data.full_name` (captured at signup, never previously read anywhere) before ever letting the model guess, plus an explicit rule 10 telling it to use exactly what's given, never a placeholder like "Your Name" or "Ayn User". Re-verified live after the fix: `basics.name` correctly came back as the real signup name.

Verified live end to end with a real throwaway account with zero resume: guided interview (a real barista role with three achievement bullets, one real associate degree, six real skills) correctly extracted into the canonical profile with every figure preserved exactly (e.g. "cut weekly waste by about 20 percent" survived unchanged) and zero invented facts; credit gate correctly refused Generate at 6 credits (`402 insufficient_credits`) and correctly charged 15 on a topped-up balance; the generated resume scored 95/100, opened its summary with the real title ("Barista with experience in..."), and named zero skills that weren't in the six given. Also fixed, found only because the live-rendered dialog surfaced them: three em dashes in `GuidedIntake.tsx`'s own user-facing copy, violating this file's own "no em dashes" rule from the moment the file was written. `npx tsc --noEmit` clean, `check-wiring` clean (13 hub actions, up from 11). Test account fully erased after via `erase_account_core`.

EVERY SIGNATURE LINE ACROSS EVERY EMAIL NOW OPENS WITH "SINCERELY,". `_shared/emailTemplate.ts`'s `signatureBlock()`/`wrapEmail()` default (`send-email`, `stripe-webhook`, `resume-hub`'s `notifyCandidate`/`notifyOrgMembers`) went from `["The AYN Team"]` to `["Sincerely,", "The AYN Team"]`. `admin-inbox-reply`'s four per-identity signatures (support, hello, ghazi, admin) each gained a leading `'Sincerely,'` line, so Ghazi's own three-line sign-off is now four lines: "Sincerely,", "GHAZI ALDHYAEI", "Founder & AI Engineer | AYN", "Land your dream job, without the burnout." `auth-send-email` and `admin-broadcast`'s standalone, non-shared signature copies got the same line added by hand, matching how the v3.115.0 signature rollout itself touched these six functions individually. All six redeployed live. Verified every real caller of `wrapEmail`/`signatureBlock` passes no explicit signature array (confirmed via grep), so none bypass the new default.

Preceded by v3.118.0, asked directly whether the "fixed" ticket reply from v3.117.0 actually reaches anyone, since there is no ticket UI, only a contact form, and a reply should be an email from support.

CORRECT — THE v3.117.0 FIX MADE THE WRITE SUCCEED, BUT NOTHING EVER TOLD THE PERSON WHO WROTE IN. Confirmed by reading the actual reachable code before writing anything: no page anywhere in this app reads `ticket_messages` back to whoever opened a ticket, guest or signed in — there is no "my tickets" view — and `TicketForm.tsx`'s only email call (`send-ticket-notification`) fires once, to notify support of a *new* ticket, never again for a reply. `admin_insert_ticket_message`'s v3.117.0 fix was real and necessary (it was erroring on every call), but even working correctly it only ever wrote a row an admin could see and nobody else could. A `send-ticket-reply` function already existed live under that exact slug, but it predated this product's entire current email era: SMTP instead of Resend, `info@aynn.io` instead of `ayn.careers`, the pre-rebrand black-and-white template, CORS still locked to `aynn.io`, and zero callers anywhere in `src/` — it could not have worked even if something had called it.

Rewrote `send-ticket-reply` from scratch on the current shared branding (`_shared/emailTemplate.ts`, the same building blocks `admin-inbox-reply` already proved out) and made it the one place a reply actually happens: admin-role check, the `ticket_messages` insert and the `support_tickets` status bump (both direct via its own service client, not a second RPC round trip), then a real email to `support_tickets.guest_email` — which `TicketForm.tsx` has always populated with the *signed-in* submitter's own account email too, not only a true guest's, so this single field covers both cases with no branching needed. Every attempt logs to `email_logs` regardless of outcome, and a failed send never undoes the already-saved reply (returns `emailed:false` instead of a hard error), matching this codebase's own standing "a notification failure must never erase real work that already succeeded" rule. `SystemPanes.tsx`'s `SupportPane.send()` now calls this function instead of the bare RPC; `admin_insert_ticket_message` itself is untouched and still correct, just no longer the caller's only stop.

Verified without emailing anyone real: a synthetic ticket with `guest_email` on `@example.com`, replied to as a genuine throwaway admin — the reply landed in `ticket_messages` with the right sender, the ticket flipped to `in_progress`, and Resend's own clean domain rejection was captured honestly in `email_logs` (`emailed:false`, the real 422 reason attached), proving the whole pipeline runs end to end. The actual template was separately rendered as a real page through a temporary, since-deleted preview function — correct logo, correct greeting, correct signature. `npx tsc --noEmit` clean, deployed live, all synthetic data and the test admin account fully erased after.

Preceded by v3.117.0, asked directly to test the admin panel end to end. Two real, live, broken admin RPCs found and fixed: replying to a support ticket and changing a ticket's status both errored on every attempt.

A FULL RPC-LEVEL PASS OVER EVERY ADMIN CAPABILITY, NOT A UI CLICK-THROUGH. The admin app's own Supabase client is deliberately built with `persistSession: false` (`src/admin-app/adminSupabase.ts`) so an admin session never survives a reload — a real security choice, not an oversight — which also means the same localStorage session-injection trick that works for the main app's `supabase` client (`persistSession: true`) does nothing here: the login screen has to be reached through its own real email/password form. That form's password field is exactly the kind of field this session's own tooling refuses to type into, the same wall this file has documented hitting more than once before (v3.86.0, v3.106.0). Rather than stop there, this round tested at the layer underneath the UI instead: a real throwaway admin-flagged account, its real JWT minted via the real `/auth/v1/token` endpoint (the same mechanism `LoginScreen`'s `signInWithPassword` itself calls), then every `get_admin_*`/`admin_*` RPC and every admin edge function (`admin-auth-pin`, `admin-broadcast`, `admin-inbox-reply`) called directly and for real, exactly the way the panel's own `adminRpc()` wrapper does.

TWO REAL, LIVE BUGS FOUND, BOTH IN THE SUPPORT TICKET FLOW. `admin_insert_ticket_message` (the Support pane's reply box) inserted into a column literally named `content`; `ticket_messages`' real column has always been `message` — every real admin reply attempt has been erroring `42703, column "content" of relation "ticket_messages" does not exist` since whenever this function was written. `admin_update_ticket` (the same pane's status/priority controls) compared `p_data->>'status'` (text) against the `status` column (the `support_ticket_status` enum) inside a bare `COALESCE`, which Postgres refuses outright — `42804, COALESCE types text and support_ticket_status cannot be matched` — so marking a ticket resolved or changing its priority has never worked either. Both fixed: the insert now targets the real `message` column with `p_sender` cast to `ticket_sender_type`, and the update casts both sides of each `COALESCE` to their real enum types. Verified live against a synthetic seeded ticket: both calls failed with the exact errors above before the fix, both succeeded (a real reply row written, status flipped to `resolved`) immediately after, with zero other code touched.

EVERYTHING ELSE HELD, NO OTHER BUGS.
